# Background

The Renewable Energy Performance Platform (REPP Phase 1P) is an existing climate finance facility for small-scale on-grid-connected and distributed renewable energy projects in sub-Saharan Africa.

Operational since 2016, REPP Phase 1 is grant-funded through the United Kingdom (UK) Foreign Commonwealth & Development Office (FCDO). The UK FCDO's initial commitment of £8m was followed by a commitment of €40m from the FCDO and as part of the programme, Camco Management Limited (Camco) is investment manager for REPP Phase 1.

# Introducing REPP2

By launching a new Programme entitled REPP2, Camco is seeking to build on the successful track record (REPP Phase 1) described in Annex A and scale up support for small-scale renewable energy power generation in key African markets, focusing on Lead Developing Countries (LDCs).

REPP 2 is a blended finance facility with a goal to create a paradigm shift for the small-scale renewable energy sector. REPP2 will focus on climate change adaptation and climate-resilient power generation, with increased access for local and vulnerable populations to modern renewable energy services.

REPP targets funding and support to smaller-scale renewable energy projects and businesses in the LDCs of sub-Saharan Africa. To be eligible for receipt of GCF Proceeds, investments must be located in a jurisdiction that is a Lead Developing Country (LDC) as defined by the World Bank Group. Nine priority countries, seven of which are LDCs, have been selected for GCF funding, including: Cameroon, Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Lesotho, Madagascar, Malawi, Ivory Coast, Nigeria, Sierra Leone and Zambia.

# Climate change problem

Many countries in sub-Saharan Africa are highly vulnerable to climate change. Drivers of this vulnerability include:

- Existing climate hazards (droughts, floods, extreme temperatures, etc.)
- High dependence on rain-fed subsistence agriculture for a largely rural population across the region
- High levels of poverty and food insecurity; and
- Weak institutional capacity (including limited access to water, sanitation, education), which often leads to high levels of national and regional conflict.

As a result, only 10% of the region's population (and even less in rural areas) have very limited capacity to adapt to climate change. The proportion of this is that fewer than half of the population of the region (estimated at 570 million people) has access to electricity. In rural areas, this proportion falls to 25%.

# Additional barriers

## Climate benefits

REPP 2 will deliver climate impact by:

- Financing renewable energy generation (Outcome 1) which contributes to reduced GHG emissions from renewable energy installations (Outcomes 1) aligned with Host Country priorities.
- Delivering improved climate resilience of energy infrastructure (Outcome 2) through diversification and decentralisation of renewable energy generation.
- Delivering greater access to clean energy for households, businesses, and communities (Outcome 3) through access to clean energy, which improves livelihoods and builds gender equity. Achievements will be particularly significant in the context of REPP 1 financing renewable energy generation, particular at the level of the underlying assets funded by REPP 2, which benefits local developers, builds gender equity and establishes a more commercially viable market for smaller-scale renewables.
- Contributing to stronger technology deployment, innovation and market transformation (Outcome 4) in line with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), particularly SDG7 on clean energy.

All of the targeted climate impacts of REPP 2 are closely aligned to the national priorities of Host Countries for mitigation and adaptation, including those defined in their Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) and National Adaptation Plans (NAPs).

6. REPP 2 financing and support

REPP 2 will further catalyze the growth of sub-Saharan Africa’s small-scale renewable energy sector by scaling up the success of REPP Phase 1, further leveraging private sector capital flows into small-scale and distributed renewable energy markets across the region, with a focus on LDCs. The program is expected to generate significant economic benefits in terms of job creation, increased access to clean energy, GHG emission reductions and provide new or improved access to clean energy to more than 7.7 million people and 10 million households in sub-Saharan Africa over a 20-year total lifespan.

REPP 2 financing and support mechanisms include:

- Early-stage and development funding, which includes early-stage and growth funding for off-grid companies (including solar home system and mini-grid companies) to demonstrate that selected technologies are commercially viable. This will also provide the necessary financial resources to demonstrate that innovative new business models are viable and sustainable. Additional development funding is provided in the form of grants to support the development of a pipeline.
- Gap funding for construction and implementation of grid-connected projects to ensure generation capacity is available at the time of project implementation. REPP 2 will also provide access to and financial risk mitigation to ensure bankability of projects, and
- Technology deployment funding to support the scaling up of new technologies, build skills and capacity among local businesses and developers, improve the ability of businesses and developers to deliver gender equity, and improve their ability to manage environmental and social governance (ESG) risks.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Climate context


#### 1. Climate change problem statement

Climate change hazards

People and communities across sub-Saharan Africa are exposed to a wide and expanding range of climate change-related hazards. The climate hazards for each REPP2 Host Country is provided below and discussed in greater detail in Annex 2.

- **Cameroon**: Hazards include drought, heavy rainfall events, flooding, heavy winds, high temperatures, heatwaves, and sea-level rise. Vulnerability to precipitation, extreme rainfall and flooding, as well as drought, and sea-level rise.
  
- **DR Congo**: Hazards include intense rainfall, erosion, floods, heatwaves, and sea-level rise. Resource on the land is limited, which makes the country vulnerable to climate change and limits the capacity of the population.

- **Lesotho**: Hazards include more frequent, severe droughts and increased flooding. The energy infrastructure in Lesotho is expected to be impacted by climate change, as well as the impact it has on subsistence agriculture significantly increases the climate vulnerability of the rural population; Over-reliance on hydropower.

- **Madagascar**: Hazards include intensification of cyclones and associated flooding, sea-level rise, intense rainfall events, droughts, heatwaves, and extreme temperatures. The energy sector is vulnerable to a reduction in precipitation and cyclones destroying infrastructure. Over-reliance on hydropower.

- **Malawi**: Hazards include floods, droughts, strong winds, intense rainfall, riverine and flash floods and tropical cyclones. Vulnerabilities include poverty, rapid population growth, dependence on the agricultural sector for livelihoods, and limited institutional capacity to manage climate change.

- **Niger**: Hazards include extreme heatwaves, increased frequency and intensity of extreme events, increased frequency of erratic rainfall and extreme droughts. Vulnerabilities include lack of financing, rapid population growth, and low institutional capacity. The energy sector in Niger is expected to be impacted by climate change as well as the impact it has on subsistence agriculture.

- **Nigeria**: Hazards include extreme heatwaves and rainfalls events leading to droughts, floods, erosion, as well as sea-level rise. Vulnerabilities include poverty, rapid population growth, dependence on the agricultural sector for livelihoods, and limited institutional capacity to manage climate change; as well as vulnerability to increased demand for cooling and heating and already experiencing multiple supply shocks.

- **Sierra Leone**: Hazards include floods, droughts, coastal erosion, decreasing river flow, storm surges, flash floods, sea-level rise, and high winds. Vulnerabilities include water resources and a high dependence on the agricultural sector for livelihoods.

- **Zambia**: Main hazards includes floods and wildfires, with droughts also increasingly common. The frequency of extreme weather events is increasing, which has a significant impact on Zambia's economy, its people's livelihoods (nearly 60% of Zambia's people earn less than the international poverty line, and three quarters of the poor live in rural areas), and the sustainability of its natural resource base.

# Climate change vulnerabilities

The sub-Saharan Africa region as a whole is generally highly vulnerable to climate change. There are several mutually reinforcing drivers of this vulnerability, including:

- Exposure to pre-existing climate hazards, as outlined above.
- High levels of poverty and food insecurity: More than one-third (417 million people in 2017) of the global population lives on less than $1.90 per day in sub-Saharan Africa. Levels of inequality across the region are also pronounced and have widened this century. In terms of food insecurity alone, the number of undernourished people has increased by more than 5 million since the COVID-19 pandemic and war in Ukraine have exacerbated food insecurity across the region.
- High dependence on agriculture: Agriculture is a major sector in sub-Saharan Africa. More than 95% of agricultural production in sub-Saharan Africa is rainfed. More than 70% of the region's total population is dependent on agriculture for their livelihoods, the majority of whom are smallholder farmers and pastoralists.
- Energy poverty: Only about half (48%) of the Host Countries have access to electricity. Of the 760 million people globally, who do not have access to electricity, 568 million (77%) are located in the region and only 25% of people living in rural areas as well.
- Vulnerability of energy infrastructure to climate hazards and climate change impacts: Climate-related disasters such as floods, droughts, and heatwaves can damage or destroy energy infrastructure. The occurrence of droughts is vulnerable to climate change. Projected decreases in annual rainfall and increases in the frequency and intensity of severe droughts exacerbate the vulnerability of energy infrastructure. In addition, extreme weather events are anticipated to lead to reduction in plant efficiencies. Given that many national grids are small and interconnected with neighboring countries as well as transmission and distribution infrastructures are often vulnerable to climate extremes.
- Vulnerability to basic human rights (including limited access to water, sanitation, education), etc.
- High levels of gender-based violence, as well as national and regional conflict, which may be exacerbated by the impacts of climate change and competition for resources.

As a result, the majority of the region's population, particularly those living in rural areas, have very limited capacity to adapt to climate change.

2. Climate rationale for mitigation

Despite being among the least contributors to global GHG emissions, sub-Saharan Africa has already experienced significant loss and damage due to climate change, with impacts including biodiversity loss, water shortages, reduced food production, reduced economic growth and even less of lives3. Power generation in sub-Saharan Africa is largely based on biomass and diesel generators.

The potential of the REPP 2 Programme to mitigate GHG emissions lies in:

* Replacement of fossil fuel-intensive grid electricity with renewable energy power generation (through large mini-grids (isolated grids) and solar photovoltaic mini-grid systems which typically replace candles, kerosene and diesel power generation).
* Replacement of fossil fuel-intensive off-grid electricity with renewable energy power generation, through large mini-grids projects.
* Providing access to reliable and affordable electricity for households and communities that have historically had little or no electricity access, through off-grid solar home systems and solar photovoltaic mini-grid systems (which typically replace candles, kerosene and diesel power generation).

The REPP 2 Programme will avoid and reduce greenhouse gas emissions (MRIA) and promote a paradigm shift for climate mitigation in all of Host Countries. The Programme will be aligned with the principles for international cooperation on climate change contained in Article 4 of the UNFCCC.

* Emission reduction activities supported by REPP 2 will be strongly aligned with priorities defined in the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) of all Host Countries.
* At the same time, REPP 2 will support countries to develop and adopt impact boundaries, GHG emission baselines using standard emission factors that are aligned to national GHG reporting, projections and monitoring systems, and to use internationally recognized/accepted Climate Development Mechanism (CDM) methodologies (as presented in Annexure Z.2B).
* The same CDM methodologies will be used to establish monitoring protocols; and
* A robust and comprehensive measurement, reporting and verification (MRV) system for GHG emission reductions has been prepared in accordance with international best practice and GCF requirements.

3. Climate rationale for adaptation

The combination of hazards, exposure, vulnerability to current climate variability and existing and limited adaptive capacity is expected to increase the risk of adverse impacts on households and communities across sub-Saharan Africa highly vulnerable to climate change. The mutually reinforcing vulnerabilities are likely to be exacerbated by climate change, unless significant investments in building adaptive capacity and making building climate resilience a priority at all Host Country NDCs and National Adaptation Plans (NAPs).

Without increasing adaptation capacity, climate impact changes are anticipated to deteriorate in all Host Countries, including decreased food security and further loss of livelihoods. Climate change hazards are also expected to increase the vulnerability of households and communities that already face considerable groups.

The potential of REPP 2 is increase climate change resilience and adaptive capacity lies in:

* The use of multiple energy sources and technologies to make energy systems more climate resilient against system disruption. For example, hybridizing hydropower generation (which is often

seasonal variability and may be negatively impacted by reduced rainfall due to climate change) with solar or wind generation can provide a buffer against climate variable;

Decentralizing and diversifying energy supply with renewable energy will enable off-grid solutions, such as solar home systems, to provide electricity for lighting, cooking, and mobile and backup grid-supplied disruptions;

Increasing access to renewable energy in off-grid communities to provide households and businesses with solutions that help build resilience and respond to the impacts of climate change. Such solutions include: providing clean cooking solutions; improving water supply and sanitation; reducing injury; electricity provision to refugee camps that are accommodating displaced people.; electricity provision to educational facilities to improve awareness and knowledge about climate change, including through the use of solar-powered mobile libraries; providing access to information enabling people and communities to benefit from access to climate early-warning systems for disaster risk reduction; improving food security by increasing the resilience of smallholder farmers in terms of management of crops and livestock; providing power for increased cooling need; irrigation to increase agricultural productivity; and providing electricity for improved water supply solutions, such as those being provided improving cooking solutions to households;

Supporting renewable energy projects that will make the sector become financially sustainable, thereby making the total renewable energy sector (as well as local economies more broadly) more resilient to macroeconomic shocks, and

Supporting technological innovations that build the capacity of households and communities to anticipate and respond to climate impacts, including innovations for the delivery of climate services (e.g., improved weather forecasts, early warning systems, agrometeorological reports and early warning systems), market information, and other information on climate change, its impacts and the solutions, as well as technological innovations for productive use applications.

Please see Tables 1 and 2 below which present REPP 2 eligible adaptation responses. Detailed country level data is provided in Annex 2.

The REPP 2 Programme responds directly to the urgent and immediate needs of vulnerable countries, as documented by NDCs. The Programme focuses on nine Host Countries in sub-Saharan Africa, of which seven are LDCs. The potential of REPP 2 is assist these countries to adapt to the impacts of climate change in terms of promoting sustainable development and a paradigm shift will be confirmed through the following:

* **Identification:** People, communities and energy infrastructure in all Host Countries and across the sub-Saharan Africa region.

* **Response:** The Programme will target adaptation impacts through increasing the resilience of the most vulnerable people and communities (AR41), in addition to energy infrastructure and the built environment.

* **Alignment:** REPP 2 will place a strong focus on aligning its investments with national-level climate, energy and development priorities. The alignment of REPP 2’s investment priorities at Programme level (improved resilience of energy infrastructure and local populations) are closely aligned with and strongly support national plans and climate strategies of the Host Countries. This is ensured through engagement with relevant government agencies, as well as other relevant non-governmental stakeholders, and confirmed through securing Non-Objection Letters from all Host Countries.

* **Monitoring and evaluation:** A comprehensive and detailed monitoring and evaluation framework has been developed for REPP 2 as part of the Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) to demonstrate its impact over time, as is expected to be approved by the general partner of Camco REPP SC5s.

# Eligibility criteria for programmatic adaptation projects

The REPP 2 Programme is cross-cutting, targeting a 20% overall contribution to climate change adaptation and 80% contribution to mitigation. Interventions may be classified as contributing to climate change adaptation or climate change mitigation.

## Step 1: Identify climate hazards and climate change impacts
The ADAPT tool is integrated within the existing analytical framework which drives eligibility, selection and assessment of projects for their contribution towards a climate change adaptation response. The ADAPT tool will provide analytical guidelines, including clear exclusion criteria and minimum criteria for acceptability. Assessment of an intervention’s eligibility as an adaptation response will be based on the extent to which it contributes to an investment's eligibility as an adaptation response as well as the nature and extent of that contribution.

## Step 2: Define adaptation response
Metrics, targets and monitoring and evaluation arrangements must be considered as part of the evaluation. Adaptation responses are expected to align with the REPP 2 Programme’s climate change adaptation targets, which will be provided by the REPP 2 Investment Committee.

Further details for the process for classifying and investing appropriately as an adaptation response are provided in Annex 1. The ADAPT tool is also used for on-site assessment of three case studies from the REPP Phase I portfolio shown in Annex 27.


## Step 3: Identify adaptation metrics and monitoring, reporting and evaluation framework

Most African countries (as illustrated in Annex 2) have low electricity rates, high energy demand and infrastructure dependence on diesel generation for sites not connected to the grid. As a baseline for the REPP 2 Programme, eligible adaptation responses for improving the climate resilience of existing generation infrastructure are presented in Table 1 (after Johnstone et al., 2013). These include measures that can be implemented at low cost and with improved resilience for people and communities as shown in Table 2. Further detail is provided in the ADAPT Analysis Guide.

| Energy subsector | Baseline | Climate hazards | Climate change impacts | Eligible adaptation response | Renewable energy technology | Metric monitored |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Hydro power generation | Low electricity rates, high energy demand and infrastructure dependence on diesel generation for sites not connected to the grid. | Increasing temperatures and evaporative losses | Reduction in renewable generation capacity or output | Increase connected renewable generation | Grid-connected solar photovoltaic (PV) | Number of new plants generating using renewable energy |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 13

## 1. Goal statement

REPP 2 will aim to create a paradigm shift by leveraging additional private sector funding to provide investments in small-scale renewable energy projects in LDCs and other sub-Saharan African countries. As a result, the most vulnerable communities in these countries will have improved access to clean energy, improved access to climate resilient solutions and information, and Host Countries will transition towards low-emission resilient development pathways, meeting their NDC targets. Additionally, the REPP 2 Programme will contribute to the achievement of SDG7, as the overall climate resilience of the energy infrastructure is enhanced, and climate resilience of vulnerable people and communities will be improved.

## 2. Paradigm shift potential

If the proposed programme receives GCF funding and leverages further private sector and commercial finance for host populations of some of the world’s most vulnerable communities will have improved access to clean energy, improved access to climate resilient solutions and information, and Host Countries will transition towards low-emission resilient development pathways, meeting their NDC targets. The increased investment in GHG emissions from electricity generation will be reduced; climate resilience of energy infrastructure will be enhanced, and climate resilience of vulnerable people and communities will be improved.

The REPP 2 activities are anticipated to lead to more skilled and experienced local businesses and project developers, which in turn will develop with the help of REPP 2 funding more financially sustainable, innovative business models. These include developing new financing solutions for renewable energy projects; building local developer capacities, as well as de-risking and innovating financial solutions which enable investment in small-scale renewable energy projects. This will contribute to a more stable and resilient private sector finance, contributing to stronger business environment and leading to more technology transfer.

The resulting increases in renewable energy capacity will lead to cleaner production of energy; reduced GHG emissions, and greater energy security due to hybridisation of energy systems that are vulnerable to climate hazards, and by decentralising energy systems. Transmission and distribution which is likely to be improved as a result of the REPP 2 Programme will also contribute to more reliable power supply, leading to lead to improved resilience to disasters through an improved ability to provide localised power solutions and to reduce vulnerability to climate change.

Furthermore, project developers are anticipated to provide electricity not only for off-grid household basic electricity use, but also to critical services (e.g. clinics, water pumping stations), cooling services (e.g. refrigeration). Information services (e.g. cell phone towers, radio) and productive use of energy opportunities

## 3. Activities and outputs delivered by REPP 2

The activities and outputs delivered by REPP 2 are designed to strengthen the market for small-scale renewables in sub-Saharan Africa and crowd in private and commercial capital leading to GHG mitigation and adaptation benefits for people and energy infrastructure. This is achieved through an integrated suite of interventions, including:

### Early-stage development funding

#### Activity 1: Provide early-stage growth funding for off-grid companies
REPP 2 will fund the early-stage development activities of companies in the renewable energy sector, notably solar home system businesses and mini-grid developers with a gender lens. This means funding of feasibility studies, ESAs, design and implementation of SMSs, and business plans, as well as lending to acquire first customers and secure inventory.

#### Activity 2: Provide development funding for co-finance
REPP 2 will provide co-finance for the early-stage development activities of companies in the renewable energy sector. This includes feasibility studies, ESAs, design and implementation of SMSs, legal due diligence, and gender action plans. All funding provided will be done with a gender lens perspective, with the view to target that a minimum 30% of REPP 2 funding is invested in women-owned companies and/or companies that provide solutions to women as energy end users in line with the 2GL GLO.

The two early-stage development funding activities delivered by REPP 2 will ensure increased technical and commercial viability (Output 1), leading to an increase in renewable energy capacity and generation (Output 2) as more projects reach financial close, are commercially viable, and become operational. Financial and social studies, gender and social studies, and gender and social studies and gender and social sound projects and ensure gender equity through increased awareness and action (Output 3).

#### Activity 3: Finance expansion of SMS businesses
REPP 2 will provide equity or loans to fund scale up of full-scale SMSs. This will help to ensure that SHS business achieves short- and long-term commercial viability (Output 1) and will help to ensure that SHS business achieves short- and long-term commercial viability (Output 1) and will help to ensure that SHS business achieves short- and long-term commercial viability (Output 1). Funding provided from a gender lens, with the view to target that at least 30% of REPP 2 funding is provided to women-owned and led companies, as well as companies that provide solutions to women as energy end users in line with the 2GL GLO. This will ensure increased technical and commercial viability (Output 1), leading to an increase in renewable energy capacity and generation (Output 2) and leading to gender equity (Output 3).

#### Activity 4: Finance construction of isolated portfolios
REPP 2 will provide financing for construction for isolated grids that can be in both peri-urban and rural areas as well as for isolated grid projects, which are typically large installations (serving entire towns and tens or hundreds of thousands of beneficiaries). Funding provided from a gender lens, with the view to target that at least 30% of REPP 2 funding will be provided in the form of loans or equity, will demonstrate that more small-scale renewable energy projects can achieve technical and commercial viability (Output 1) and ensure increased renewable energy capacity when more mini-grids and isolated grids are constructed and become operational (Output 2). REPP 2 will

be aligned with the G2 GLI criteria, which means that it will target 30% of funding is provided to women-owned and led companies, as well as companies that provide solutions to women as energy end users to deliver greater gender equality (Output 5).

### Gap funding for construction and implementation of grid-connected projects

**Activity 5:** Provide gap funding for construction of grid-connected projects. REPP 2 will provide gap funding for the construction of renewable projects with a gender lens, which means that both women and men are involved in the project. REPP 2 will demonstrate that more grid-connected small-scale renewable energy projects can achieve technical and commercial viability (Output 1) and are constructed and become operational leading to increased renewables capacity (Output 3). REPP 2 will also provide gap funding for the development of innovative financial structures and traditional financing approaches to reduce cost and complexity for developers and sponsors in bringing their projects to market. This will be done by providing a more limited set of agreements, such as a simplified loan package or a smaller scale of REPP 2 projects. In practice this requires careful consideration of the project’s risk profile and financial structure. REPP 2 will also provide gap funding for the development of innovative financial structures that close the financial gaps that currently prevent the deployment of small-scale renewables. For example, REPP 2 will consider taking a more limited set of direct agreements, proceeding with a limited or even no government guarantee, and providing a simplified loan package.

### Activity 6: Support the provision of financial risk mitigation to ensure appropriate and cost-effective risk mitigation

REPP 2 will innovate in terms of its approach to funding and mitigating risk in HS businesses (Activities 1-5). REPP 2 will provide support for developing innovative approaches to managing and designing new risk mitigation strategies, including minimum revenue guarantees (in partnership with key stakeholders) and other financial instruments. REPP 2 will also provide gap funding for public and private sector finance into these sectors. In addition, REPP 2 will be the provider of the promotion of and access to co-investment facilities in this market segment. REPP 2 will also encourage its investors to secure such facilities. For example, REPP 2 will employ a risk-sharing mechanism with other investors to ensure that cost-effective financial, technical and political risk mitigation solutions can be offered for projects.

### Technology deployment, innovation and market transformation

**Activity 7:** Provide targeted support to developers. REPP 2 will provide tailored support and capacity building to developers and entrepreneurs as part of the investment process. Tailored support and capacity building will include training in gender equality, financial literacy, business planning, environmental and social management. This support will improve skills and expertise of project developers and original companies in the small-scale renewables sector across sub-altar markets, thus supporting market transformation.

## Activity 9: Non-financial support to promote gender equity

In addition to the investments which will be aligned with the 2X GLI criteria, REPP 2 investee companies will be required to establish gender action plans within their business strategies and operations. These plans should include a clear commitment to gender equality in all aspects of the investment, as well as more gender balanced stakeholder and community engagement and land acquisition. Further detail on the gender-disaggregated impacts and indicators are provided in Section D.1 and Annex 8.

Further details on all activities are provided in Section B.3 (Item 2: REPP 2 activities).

## Key barriers and risks for climate outcomes

The following key barriers and risks which may prevent the realisation of REPP 2 outcomes have been identified:

- **Uncertainties and delays in implementing policy and regulatory frameworks** and/or limited agreement amongst public sector stakeholders with implementation of new or untested regulatory frameworks. This can lead to increased transaction costs, delayed project development, reduced investor confidence, higher risk premiums, and ultimately lower investment volumes.
- **Political instability**, including changes in government policies, political violence, and corruption, can also impact REPP 2 investments by increasing transaction costs, delaying project development, reducing investor confidence, and raising risk premiums. This may lead to lower investment volumes or even complete abandonment of projects.
- **Limited bankability for on-grid utilities** in Host Countries in which the national grid is not sufficiently developed or reliable enough to support large-scale renewable energy projects. This can limit co-investor appetite for smaller renewables sector and hinder a project reaching financial close.
- **Limited access to finance at both the fund level and the level of underlying projects**. At the fund level, there are currently no dedicated funds available that specifically target REPP 2 investments. At the project level, public and private investors typically seek risk mitigation through the capital structure of a fund, requiring co-investment from other sources such as development finance institutions (DFIs), infrastructure funds as well as private and commercial investors, which means that such investors face the risk of not achieving financial close. As a consequence, REPP 2 may struggle to attract sufficient investment volumes.
- **Limited access to finance for small and medium-sized businesses** which provide tailored energy services and innovative financing models for residual productive uses of electricity and last mile customers. Smaller project sizes limit generation and revenue potential. Costs of development and growth are also higher per unit of capacity than larger projects, leading to increased risk premiums.
- **Macroeconomic risks**, including high inflation rates, exchange rate volatility, and high interest rates have emerged due to high inflationary growth conditions in local economies which can lead to reduced investment volumes or even complete abandonment of projects. Macroeconomic risks as interest rates are raised to combat inflation and protect growth.

## Mitigation measures

REPP 2 will continue to provide best ESG practice and gender equality. The level of environmental and social safeguards, their operationalisation and monitoring in many cases is a key factor for the success of investments. This includes effective risk management, prevention, labour rights and occupational health and safety, as well as gender balanced consultation and implementation. This may create an environment where investees is not motivated to uphold the required standards.

- Limited ability and appetite from investors to improve their ESG practice and gender balance: Investors may consider this out of their core focus, time-consuming and costly without understanding the importance for risk mitigation and benefit of creating sustainable projects and businesses.
- Furthermore, women are often excluded from decision-making processes in energy sectors. Women’s rights and the rights of women – notably with respect to land ownership - are frequently not respected, which is likely to result in a lack of gender balance in project teams.

- Limited capacity of grids and grid operators to manage variable renewable energy supply which may arise due to lack of grid connectivity between high renewable resource regions to high power demand centers. This could lead to increased costs for grid infrastructure upgrades or increased project costs due as grid infrastructure needs to be upgraded to enable projects to feed in.
The following external assumptions, which cannot be controlled by REPP 2, have been made for a successful project:
1. Sufficient supportive policy and regulatory frameworks exist or can be developed and there is political will to do so;
2. Public and private investors are willing to invest in jurisdictions where REPP 2 operates;
3. Jurisdictions in which REPP 2 operates and remain politically sufficiently stable for renewable energy projects.
4. For grid-connected projects, utilities are bankable and offtake risks can be managed through available mechanisms.
5. Sufficient demand for and opportunities exist to support women-owned and led businesses.

B.3 Project/programme description

# 1. The REPP 2 Programme

REPP 2 will finance and promote the growth of small to medium-sized renewable energy projects across sub-Saharan Africa. Through this process, REPP 2 will catalyse the growth of sub-Saharan Africa's smaller-scale renewable energy sector, which is critical for building climate resilience for people, communities and energy infrastructure. Building on the success of REPP Phase 1, REPP 2 will focus on support in nascent renewable energy markets to help them overcome barriers that are preventing their development. REPP 2 will aim to directly reduce or avoid more than 12.7 m tCO2e of GHG emissions, provide new or improved energy access to more than 30 million people and improve the quality of life for 3.75 million people and improve the resilience of nearly one million beneficiaries.

In addition to reducing GHG emissions through renewables, REPP 2 will deliver adaptation impacts and co-benefits by providing projects across sub-Saharan Africa with climate-smart solutions, as well as diversifying and decentralising renewable energy infrastructure across sub-Saharan Africa. All of REPP's targeted climate impacts are closely aligned with the national priorities of Host Countries for mitigation and adaptation in line with their Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs).

REPP 2 will fund both on-grid and off-grid renewable energy projects and companies, including isolated load developments in which renewable energy generation is used as the source of power for entire towns and small communities. REPP 2 will focus on providing access to affordable, reliable, sustainable, modern, clean, often first-time affordable power to tens of thousands of beneficiaries through one installation alone. REPP 2 will also provide additional funding to support projects that are in the early stages of development or have additional public and private funding into the smaller renewables sector. The Programme will focus on LDCs, where the funding and capacity gaps are greatest.

The REPP 2 programme is a market-based approach, working directly with private sector developers and companies to help develop a viable renewable energy sector and a vibrant financing market for smaller renewable energy projects. Furthermore, REPP 2 seeks to be:

- *Adaptive*: in that it will support sectors developments would otherwise lag or not occur.
- *Transformational*: in that it has the potential to build a market ecosystem that allows the ongoing growth of small-scale and distributed renewables in the region.

REPP 2 will deliver on this mandate through providing funding and additional forms of support to developers designed specifically to overcome the barriers identified in Section B.1.


---
**Page 25**
---

2. REPP 2 activities

All REPP 2 activities fall within a single component, designed to provide funding and support for small-scale renewable businesses and projects across sub-Saharan Africa. The activities delivered by REPP 2 are designed to strengthen the market for small-scale renewables in sub-Saharan Africa and crowd in private capital leading to GHG mitigation and improved resilience of people and energy infrastructure.

The following sections below describe the provisions:

- Early-stage growth funding for off-grid electrification and development funding for grid-connected projects
- Gap financing for growth of SBS businesses as well as construction of mini-grids, isolated grids and grid-connected projects.
- Development stage funding, employment, innovation and market transformation

### Early-stage development and growth funding

A range of funding activities for REPP 2 is described below. For all equity investments, REPP 2 will typically take a minority shareholding in accordance with its Investment Policy. REPP 2 will act as an anchor investor and provide up to 50% of the total capital requirements. For debt investments, REPP 2 will typically require a minimum of 30% from the investor.

#### Activity 1: Provide early-stage growth funding for off-grid companies

REPP 2 will provide growth capital to off-grid electrification companies, many of whom are small, locally-owned businesses. REPP 2 will use a variety of equity instruments including equity-like hybrid instruments (highly subordinated debt or senior ranked equity), typically convertible notes or redeemable shares.

Identified needs for SBS businesses include smaller equity investments to demonstrate that selected technologies are fit-for-purpose and the necessity to establish innovative new business models, acquire first customers and access to finance.

Mini-grid developers require smaller equity investments to demonstrate that selected technologies are fit-for-purpose, as well as the need to establish innovative new business models, acquire first customers and access to finance.

This funding will help these businesses to achieve scale and sustainability and overcome key barriers, such as a lack of early-stage or development funding, limited access to finance and challenging economics for smaller projects.

#### Activity 2: Provide development-stage funding for grid-connected projects

REPP 2 will provide development-stage funding in the form of hybrid equity instruments that are typically either repaid at close/final sale or include an option for REPP 2 to convert the loan into funding for construction (at final sale) or a pre-sale facility. REPP 2 will also provide simplified development loans for the financing of smaller-scale projects which are lack of early-stage or development funding, and limited access to finance.

The simplified development loan terms offered by REPP 2 will also help to reduce barriers related to challenging economics for smaller projects because the costs of development are typically large relative to the revenues generated. This can result in lower revenue streams, revenue in more marginal return expectations for developers, sponsors and commercial lenders.

The simplified terms and conditions for REPP 2 development loans will also help to overcome the burdened cost and cash flow requirements. In accordance with the risk appeal of REPP 2, the development stage offers will be structured to assume equity-like risk during this development phase and will be priced to ensure

#### Activity 3: Finance separation of SHG businesses
This activity will seek to overcome barriers related to limited access to finance for SHG businesses, including a lack of early-stage or development funding, limited access to finance for growth, and a limited appetite for investment in new markets.

### Activity 4: Finance construction of mini-grid portfolios

REPP 2 may fund construction of mini-grid portfolios and isolated grid developments by way of hybrid equity investments with SHG businesses and private sector teams. This funding will be used for construction and to achieve scale and sustainability once the mini-grid business model is proven.

For mini-grid developers, this activity will help to overcome barriers which include a lack of early-stage or development funding, limited access to finance for growth, and a limited appetite for investment in new markets. Mini-grid developers face additional barriers related to priced or unrestricted domestic policy and regulatory frameworks where regulatory frameworks are new and regulated tariffs are not cost-reflective.

Activity 4 will also help overcome the Commercial Viability of off-grid businesses and grid-connected projects. Output 1 is described in detail in item B below.

Funding for the expansion of SHG businesses (Activity 3) and construction of mini-grid portfolios (Activity 4) also aims to provide access to finance for productive use of electricity, providing access to first-time electricity access for productive use of electricity and critical services (Output 2), described in further detail in item B below.

### Activity 5: Provide gas funding for construction of grid-connected projects

REPP 2 will seek to crowd in private and public sources of funding and provide additional (gap) funding to fill a confirmed gap in the capital structure and achieve financial close. REPP 2 may provide funding via hybrid equity investments with SHG businesses, private sector teams, development finance institutions (DFIs), infrastructure funds as well as private and commercial lenders.

REPP 2 will also seek to crowd in private sources of funding for grid-connected projects from small companies where the smaller project size limits generation capacity and thus revenue and return expectations. Simplified and streamlined funding conditions will also help to overcome the burden and cost of financing.

## Activity 6: Support the provision of financial risk mitigation

In nascent markets, developers of grid-connected projects face additional barriers related to limited or untested domestic policy and regulatory frameworks and need to secure power purchase agreements with grid operators. These contracts are subject to a number of risks including price volatility, energy produced and variance energy generation from renewable energy sources such as solar PV (examples of this can be found in the REPP 1 Phase 2 projects).

REPP 2 will seek to ensure appropriate and cost-effective risk mitigation for off-grid business, as well as developers and sponsors of grid-connected projects. In doing so, REPP 2 seeks to reduce generally higher transaction costs associated with financing renewable energy projects in developing countries compared to LDC markets. Elements of a more innovative approach to mitigating real and perceived risk and attracting more commercial and private sector funding include:

- Assessing the potential for co-financing with other businesses (Activity 3), mixed/multi portfolios (Activity 4) and grid-connected projects (Activity 5). To reduce currency risk for business and funders alike, REPP 2 will work with additional sources of commercial as well as public local currency financing. This will be achieved by working closely with the World Bank’s International Finance Corporation (IFC) and other development finance institutions to provide co-financing opportunities.
- Securing hybrid risk mitigation strategies for mini-grid portfolios, including minimum revenue guarantees. This new form of risk mitigation is being considered in the market but is not yet widely available. REPP 2 will work with key risk mitigation providers on the region, to devolve these investments and reduce transaction costs associated with financing mini-grids (Activity 3). REPP 2 will also promote the provision of and access to connections subsidies.
- Employing a hybrid approach to financial risk mitigation for mini-grid projects to reduce cost and complexity for developers and sponsors to bring their projects to financial close. This approach may be implemented through a combination of off-grid and grid-connected opportunities, as has already utilised in REPP Phase 1 (e.g., co-financing for off-grid projects (Activity 2) and hybrid mini-grids (Activity 5), asset finance loans with loan principal growing in line with the asset base of the company, and a combination of equity and debt financing). This approach may include a hybrid financial close or including an option for REPP 2 to convert the loan into funding for construction at financial close (Activity 2) and use of hybrid equity instruments to fund investments in the mini-grid sector (Activity 3).

The capital structure of REPP 2 has been designed to address endemic risk factors such as currency risk, which is a significant barrier to financing renewable energy projects. The project will mitigate any across the portfolio, including through defaults caused by currency devaluation and transfer and convertibility risk. This is likely to be more efficient than mitigating risk at the level of each transaction. REPP 2 will also seek to provide guarantees for financial institutions that are willing to invest in senior equity investors and noteholders. Such guarantees cover the core of risks of losses due to any form of non-performance or default by the project, including through currency devaluation.

At the transaction level, financial risk mitigation may take the form of political risk insurance, credit enhancement or mitigation of foreign exchange risk to address currency mismatches between revenues denominated in foreign currencies and costs denominated in local currencies. REPP 2 will work with networks of risk mitigation providers to reduce risks for governments, developers and funders alike. As was the case for REPP Phase 1, where a first-of-a-kind innovative risk mitigation solution for off-taker liquidity risk was adopted, REPP 2 will continue to work with its partners to develop and implement innovative risk

**B**

mitigation against Existing risk mitigation providers used by REPP 1 and likely to be used by REPP 2 including the African Trade Insurance Company (ATI), Guarantco and World Bank MIGA Continuous exchange of pipeline information and collaboration with partners are ensured through well-established communication channels. The decision on whether to use REPP 2 or other forms of risk mitigation default cover under a Power Purchase Agreement (PPA) or other forms of political risk cover will be made on a project-by-project basis, taking into account the availability of such risk mitigation instruments These decisions will be approved by the REPP Investment Committee in every case.

REPP will attempt to secure (as appropriate and as available) the following existing forms of financial risk mitigation:

- **Political risk insurance (PRI)**: PRI will be sourced at a transaction level. Typically, PRI cover will include transfer and convertibility risk; breach of contract; expropriation; and war and civil disturbance REPP 2 may also source PRI to cover other risks such as sovereign default or political violence.

- **Offshore credit risk**: Offshore credit risk is the risk that an offshore lender will not be available to extend offshorerecredit for risk national utilities. These include a) payment liquidity cover (e.g. standby letters of credit, or fully-funded letter of credit); b) currency conversion guarantee; c) political risk insurance (PRI); d) sovereign guarantees or breach of contract cover provided through PRI). REPP 2 may source payment liquidity cover from the ATI as well as Guarantco and other risk mitigation providers. Further coverage is available directly from offshores and lenders (for debt service reserve accounts). REPP 2 may source non-payment (default) cover from the ATI as well as Guarantco and other risk mitigation providers A further form of credit risk mitigation is provided by the ATI in the form of a guarantee for the payment of national utilities which are acting as the offshorere for independent power producer projects).

- **Currency conversion risk**: Currency conversion risk arises when there is a mismatch between what project economics and counterparty creditworthiness allow, currency risk may be mitigated through denominating loans and equity investments in hard currency, which transfers FX risk from REPP 2 to borrowers. REPP 2 may engage in direct currency hedging at the transition level (via foreign exchange forwards and/or currency swaps) to hedge against the risk of a mismatch between project economics and what is available to borrowers/investors but would pass the costs of that hedging on to those borrowers/investors Relative to the reduced scale of the revenues generated by smaller-scale renewable energy projects, these hedging costs are likely to be significant. REPP 2 may source currency conversion risk cover from risk mitigation providers such as TCX Other (potentially cost-effective) opportunities exist for FX hedging at the portfolio level and/or through the use of structured products which can help decrease currencies of assets in terms of costs which can pass on to borrowers/investors.

This support will be offered through eligible hybrid equity instruments or as part of subscribed or shared loss commitments at financial close Activities supported include the following:

- **Activity 7**: To overcome the limited experience and funding for local developers by engaging with them at their own pace and building local developer capacities by providing technical support to upskill project developers and off-grid companies. This activity, conducted as part of REPP investment approach,

will build project finance and specifically project finance risk management skills for local developers, enabling those developers to mitigate key risks and thereby secure both public and private sector funding. It will also support off-grid companies in strengthening their business plans and fundraising processes. Investments will also be made in the development of national renewable energy action plans and National Adaptation Plans.

Given the significance of local currency financing in providing long-term sustainable financing solutions for local projects, REPP 2 will also work to upskill and crowd in local (usually locally-currency) funders, thereby making an important contribution to market development.

**Activity 8: Improve environmental and social risk mitigation**

REPP 2 will support the design, implementation, and management of an environmental and social management system (ESMS) that is commensurate with the level of risk for that investment and is fit for purpose in accordance with the REPP's environment and social policy, which will itself be aligned with GCF and international best practice. This ESMS will also support knowledge transfer to developers in REPP 2 Host Countries.

REPP 2 will fund and progressively support the development of their ESMSs and will establish new or expand on existing **guidelines** and tools to build their capacity.

**Activity 9: Non-financial support to promote gender equity**

REPP 2 will integrate a gender equity perspective into the design, implementation, monitoring and assessment of all renewable energy activities financed by REPP 2. This involves:

- Analysing gender equity in the investor company, country of operation, targeted sector.
- Establishing a baseline against which GAFI (gender analysis framework) indicators and gender-disaggregated targets against an established baseline.
- Integrating these into the two bullet points above into a monitoring plan.

REPP 2 support for technology deployment, innovation and market transformation will build skills and capacity amongst local businesses and developers (Output 3), as described further in item 3.

### 3. REPP 2 outputs

The above activities will lead to the following REPP 2 outputs:

**Output 1: Increased commercial viability**

All REPP 2 activities, including early-stage growth and development funding, gap funding for construction and support for market development combine to demonstrate the potential for technical and commercial viability of renewable energy projects in REPP 2 Host Countries. This will be demonstrated across REPP 2 Host Countries and across the region. Over its 20-year lifespan, REPP 2 targets support for thirty-seven (37) new investments in REPP 2 countries and the full funding commitments will be disbursed within seven (7) years of the first close of REPP 2.

**Output 2: Increased renewable energy capacity and generation**

Early-stage and construction funding from REPP 2 ensures that new renewable energy capacity and generation is brought online. The total amount of renewable energy capacity financed by REPP 2 contributes directly to improved access to clean electricity for women and men and host-country priorities as outlined in their policy frameworks as well as their Nationally-Determined Contributions and National Adaptation Plans.

# Output: 3. Strengthen skills and capacity

REPP 2 support for project developers establish a more active and commercially-viable market for smaller-scale renewables, which is better-able to attract both public and private finance in future. These impacts are expected to be felt across the region, with REPP 2 also supporting the development of new markets in other emerging markets and with developers being placed better to expand to those markets. REPP 2 will directly contribute to improved gender equity through a focus on gender mainstreaming within investment projects.

## 4. REPP 2 outcomes

The activities and outputs delivered by REPP 2 are designed to strengthen the market for small-scale renewables and crowd in private capital leading in sub-Saharan Africa leading to GHG mitigation and improved and strengthened resilience energy infrastructure and vulnerable people and communities, as follows:

### Outcome 1: Reduced GHG emissions from renewable energy installations

Early-stage and development funding from REPP 2 will contribute to the achievement of the program's objective of reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions through generation capacity by helping off-grid companies to establish their business models and begin their growth towards scale, sustainability, and by helping grid-connected projects to reach financial close.

For a subset of projects, REPP 2 will provide co-financing for renewable energy installations. These projects are expected to become operational, thereby reducing or avoiding GHG emissions from fossil fuels. Targeted mitigation outcomes include 12.7M tCO2e of avoided or reduced GHG emissions and 330MW of new renewable generation capacity through the deployment of solar PV technologies.

### Outcome 2: Improved climate resilience of energy infrastructure

Funding for construction from REPP 2 contributes to the climate resilience of energy infrastructure in numerous ways (see Annex 2 for country specific details):

- **Future-proofing**: REPP 2 will support the development of renewable energy installations which offer lower-cost, climate-resilient solutions and stable sources of clean energy generation for people, communities and businesses not connected to the grid.
- **Grid stability**: Increased renewable energy generation can lead to greater grid stability and climate resilience, especially in countries where transmission and distribution infrastructure is already constrained. REPP 2 will support projects that are designed to be resilient against disruptions and transmission capacity is expected due to climate change.
- **Countries which depend on hydropower generation but are also drought-prone with high rainfall variability**: REPP 2 will support projects that can withstand decreases in annual rainfall and increases in the occurrence and severity of droughts increase climate change vulnerability. Hybrid power solutions (e.g., solar PV combined with diesel generators or pumped hydro storage infrastructure) can directly contribute to improved grid stability and greater climate resilience.

### Outcome 3: Improved climate resilience of vulnerable people and communities

Funding for off-grid electrification projects will not only provide first-time and affordable clean energy access and improve quality of life of low-income people who typically live in rural communities and are most vulnerable to climate change, but it will also build climate resilience outcomes including:

---

B

* **Communities connected to the grid often rely extensively on wood biomass, kerosene and diesel as a fuel source:** Reduced biomass availability and increase in fuel prices represent additional climate change vulnerabilities. Adoption of renewable energy solutions such as solar lighting, renewable energy powered water pumps, etc., can help reduce these risks.

* **Improved access to climate information through cellular networks powered by renewable energy improves ability to respond to extreme weather events:** Communities that are not connected to the grid but have mobile phones can then benefit from access to early-warning systems relating to both pending disasters (storm, hail, flooding risk) and seasonal climate information (relating to planning and decision-making for management of water resources). The quality of such information can be improved through solar powered cell phone charging, radio and TV.

* **Improved and more stable energy supply for critical community services such as health care facilities, emergency shelters, etc., will help communities better prepare and respond to climate risks:** Similarly, e-mobility can assist some of the most vulnerable agricultural communities to better access markets. The need for irrigation as well as cooling will increase, and solar powered solutions can help build climate resilience in water resource management.

**Outcome 3 (improved climate resilience for vulnerable people and communities) supports all Host Country adaptation and climate resilience priorities.**

The target for private and commercial finance mobilised for investment is US$393 million.

* **In addition to the above, REPP 2 intends to target up to 30% of its funding in support of 2X Challenge Gender Lens Investing (2XL) opportunities. This represents a target of USD78 million.**

**Outcome 5: Contributing to stronger technology deployment, innovation and market transformation**

* **REPP 2 will:** contribute to more and more rapid technology deployment by providing targeted support and finance to renewable energy companies during early and construction stages of the asset and by financing the deployment of new technologies in the grid-connected sector. This will also help to mainstream and facilitate first-of-a-kind transactions and business models; and enhance market readiness for REPP 2 projects through co-financing and capacity building support to local developers, based on the application of sound commercial investment principles aimed at delivering commercially viable and bankable projects. This support – directly and through demonstration effect to broader sector stakeholders – will stimulate sectoral development that would otherwise lag or

Based on the current pipeline and the target for private or commercial sources (unpublished data), based on the proven track record of REPP Phase 1, the same proportion has been applied in estimating the target for private or commercial funding mobilised in third party funding. On this basis, the target for private or commercial funding mobilised is USD930 million.**

5. REPP 2 co-benefits

The construction and operation of renewable energy power plants will create new jobs during construction and operations for 76,801 people (co-benefit 1). REPP 2 will also enhance gender equity (co-benefit 2) through:

- Activities:
    * $70 mUSD funding committed to 2X Challenge's Gender Lens Investing criteria-aligned companies
    * 11 female-led or managed companies funded by REPP 2
    * All REPP 2 investments are required to implement gender action plans to increase gender equity awareness and performance within investee company and though their project implementation. The REPP target is 37 developers with improved gender equity awareness and practices.
    * 1 million people reached through capacity building activities

REPP 2 funding and support will lead to improved quality of life through community and critical services connected to electricity (co-benefit 3). Co-benefits include improved access to healthcare, lighting, refrigeration, water supply, sanitation, and education.

These co-benefits will allow further socioeconomic development of Host Countries and their off-grid communities. The target for this co-benefit is improved quality of life through access to improved lighting for 31 million people by the end of REPP 2.

REPP 2 will deliver improved ESG practices (co-benefit 4) by providing its investees with training and funding to identify environmental and social risk and impacts, and to establish mitigation and management strategies. This will lead to improved financial performance and overall sustainability and social performance. Examples include improved waste management practices, mitigating the potential for negative impacts on biodiversity and water resources, improved labour practices and Occupational Health and Safety (OHS) standards.

6. Impact recognition

Individual projects or off-grid renewable energy companies are eligible for multiple forms of support and funding from REPP 2. For example, an off-grid company may receive both growth funding (Activity 1) and project financing (Activity 3). Similarly, a company may receive funding during the development phase (Activity 2) as well as gap financing for construction (Activity 5), or it might receive only one type of support at any given time. REPP 2 will not provide follow-on financing in these situations. Any decision to provide follow-on financing will typically not alter those metrics, with progress towards achievement of those metrics measured and reported through the project life cycle.

Where a company by REPP 2 to provide follow-on financing results in a variable change to the amount of co-financing leverage (e.g., from public-private partnerships to private sources) or a change to the planned installed capacity for a project (or mini-grid or SHS portfolio), such changes may result in a different level of support.

7. Rationales for selection of interventions

The host country feasibility studies presented in Annex 2 include a country-level needs assessment that highlights the need for funding for on- and off-grid renewables. The demand for the types of interventions outlined above is verified in Annex 2 based on reported impacts from REPP Phase 2 interventions as well as the stated pipeline of projects for REPP 2.

# 8. Eligibility criteria for REPP 2

## Installed capacity: REPP 2 will fund renewable energy power generation projects with an installed capacity of at least 50 MW (except for wind). Eligible projects can be further categorized as follows:

- Grid-connected projects (independent power producers or IPPs)
- Large, isolated grids, where power is provided to entire towns (tens of thousands of connections); and
- Off-grid RE power companies, providing mini-grid (MG) and solar home system (SHS) solutions

### Eligible technologies: As outlined in Annex 6, REPP 2 may invest in the following renewable energy power generation projects, including but not limited to:

- Wind
- Solar photovoltaic (PV)
- Small hydroelectric power
- Biomass
- Geothermal
- Tidal and wave power
- Off-grid solar home systems

### Eligible countries: REPP 2 targets small scale renewable energy power generation projects in the Least Developed Countries of sub-Saharan Africa. To be eligible for receipt of GCF Proceeds, investments must be located in Host Countries, i.e., a jurisdiction which has provided a Letter of Non-Objection in a form and substance as set out in GCF Decision 10/2014.

### REPP 2 may invest in other sub-Saharan African countries (including countries which are not classified as Least Developed Countries) where a Letter of No Objection has not been provided. Such investments will be treated under the criteria for Category B or C projects and will be ineligible to receive GCF funding.

## EAS risk categorisation: REPP 2 investments in Africa will be assessed as IPC risk category B or C. Category A projects are excluded. Please see Annex 6 for further detail.

### Investment requirements:

- When REPP 2 is making a project finance investment (typically for grid-connected IPPs), the sponsor must have at least 50% equity stake.
- Where REPP 2 depends on project IPF, it allows only (by definition, there is no recourse to the balance sheets of the sponsors) investments in projects with an assessed financial and technical viability of the project itself.

### Where REPP 2 is providing growth capital (either as debt or equity) to off-grid companies:

- The ability to repay the investment depends on the long-term sustainability of the borrower. Key evaluation criteria includes estimates of demand and revenue growth.
- In some cases, REPP 2 may invest on parent company guarantees, which are subject to a credit assessment.

### Where REPP 2 is funding development for IPP via development loans:

- Such loans are exposures to the risk of failed development. Investment criteria include a requirement for a discernible pathway to commercial and financial viability.
- The sponsor must provide security in the form of a pledge of the sponsor's shares in the project SPV as well as legal charge over project assets.

### Off-grid companies: Early-stage and growth capital for off-grid companies will be provided in the form of equity (ordinary and preference shares), converted notes, junior debt, or development loans.

### Scope of REPP 2 funding:

- Businesses which provide renewable energy generation services (e.g., solar panels, battery banks, radio and TV, mobile phone charging and lights) and other appliances, including PUE devices (cooling solutions, barbeques, kits, irrigation etc.) are eligible for REPP 2 funding.
- Businesses which do not provide RE generation devices (e.g. sub-stations, transformers or cookstoves), and business which operate as retailers or wholesalers are not eligible for REPP 2 funding.

## As further detailed in Annex 14 (Term Sheet).

## Mini-grid businesses: Mini-grid businesses must engage in the development, construction and / or operation of renewable energy generation facilities (including generation, backup and reticulation) at sites which are not initially connected to the main electricity grid. Typically, first-line connection includes lights, Solar water heating systems, small refrigerators, fans, mobile phone chargers and other appliances (such as pressure cookers, freezers), to their clients.

## On-grid projects: IPPs must engage in the development, construction and operation of renewable energy generation facilities at sites which are connected to the grid. REPP 2.0 may also fund the costs of grid connection.

## 9. Country prioritisation
Only GCF Host Countries are eligible for investment from Portfolio HoldCo S.A.r.l. These Host Countries have been prioritised based on climate change vulnerabilities and impacts outlined in Section B1. Section D outlines the process for determining country-level priorities through stakeholder consultation and by aligning their climate change mitigation and adaptation priorities with the objectives of REPP 2, as outlined in Annex A.6 as well as Annex T and Annex Z.

### Criteria for prioritisation:
- **Annex A.1 (i):** Projects will be selected based on LDC status or well demonstrated demand based on existing pipelines.
- **Annex A.1 (ii):** In all cases, projects selected align with and promote country-level priorities in accordance with government strategic objectives, as reflected in NDCs, NAPAs and other policy and planning documents.

### Given that REPP 2 is driven by market demand for funding and requested support by project developers and off-grid renewable energy companies, country prioritisation is also a function of that demand, with the following criteria being used to determine demand:

1. **Annex A.3 (i):** Projects will be selected based on LDC status or well demonstrated demand.
2. **Annex A.3 (ii):** In all cases, projects selected align with and promote country-level priorities in accordance with government strategic objectives, as reflected in NDCs, NAPAs and other policy and planning documents.

## 10. REPP 2 risk management framework
REPP 2 will incorporate a comprehensive enterprise risk management (ERM) framework. As outlined in the Operations Manual (Annex Z), throughout the investment life cycle (selection, analysis, approval, due lifecycle, implementation and exit / repayment), risk will be assessed using a risk management toolkit which includes:

- **The ADAPT tool:** used to assess a candidate investment's eligibility to be classified as a climate change adaptation (cross-cutting) investment.
  - A project assessment tool (PAT), which is used to assess a wide range of risk factors in all investments, including market, credit, financial, technical, environmental and social, and regulatory as well as legal and documentation risks.
- **Adaptive asset class management:** The use of counterparty credit risk (for off-takers and borrowers) as well as environmental and social risks (unique to each technology and asset class).

The REPP 2 risk management framework is designed to facilitate robust and dynamic risk identification, analysis, assessment, prioritisation and mitigation. It will be applied throughout the investment lifecycle from selection through exit / repayment.

As Manager of REPP 2 and REPP Phase I before that, Camco Management Limited has extensive experience in managing and mitigating risks associated with renewable energy investments across asset classes and countries in unique ways. The ERM framework and proactive risk management approach adopted by an experienced team with on-the-ground presence throughout sub-Saharan Africa means that Camco is well-placed to manage existing and emerging risks effectively.


---
**Page 35**
---

The ERM framework for REP2-2 will include a risk appetite statement, approved by the AFM, to be followed by all staff during origination, structuring and ongoing portfolio management (once investments have been made). REP2-2 will appoint a dedicated risk manager. Risk governance and oversight will be provided by AFM as supported by the Investment Committee of Camco REP SCSPs.

The ERM framework is designed to manage the potential for financial losses within the REP2-2 portfolio, remain within approved risk tolerance levels. A detailed limits framework, covering (a) counterparty concentration limits at the borrower, (b) group exposure limits and (c) market risk limits (including foreign exchange and interest rate limit) is defined in the REP2-2 Operational Annexes (Annex 21).

The enterprise-wide risk management framework, including policies, procedures, governance arrangements, risk management tools and indicative limits framework are described in the Operations Manual (Annex 21). The Operations Manuals outlines specific eligibility criteria for risk assessment procedures for dealing with the following: Group Exposure Limits, Counterparty Concentration Limits, well as Market Risk Limits (e.g. grids and grid-connected projects) and Host Country Key. Risk and risk mitigation actions for the successful implementation of REP2-2 and achievement of its goals are outlined in Section F.

### B.4. Implementation arrangements

#### Camco REP SCSPs

The main fund vehicle for REP2-2, and the primary Executing Entity (Camco REP SCSPs), is a société en commandite par actions (SCpA) or special limited partnership, established in Luxembourg under the Luxembourg law of 10 August 1915 on commercial companies (the “1915 Law”). It is subject to the provisions of the Luxembourg law.

The advantages of this structure for REP2 include:

- The Camco SCSPs will be a separate legal entity and limited liability for its limited partners, provided they do not participate in the management of the SCpA vis-à-vis third parties (such management included being a member of the board of directors).
- It enables a flexible structure that can fully accommodate the blended capital structure of REP2-2.
- The structure is market-tested and widely used for similar pooled investment structures and attractive to existing investors of REP2-2.
- Governance can be adapted to reflect both the fiduciary standards of the GCF and any requirements of other limited partners.

Based in Luxembourg, Camco SCSPs will be an alternative investment fund or “AIF” for the purposes of the Luxembourg law. The Camco SCSPs is subject to the supervision of the AFM. Compliance with AFMD can be expected to provide additional comfort to EUKOK and other investors.

The general terms of Camco SCSPs will be Camco REP GP Sàrl, a Luxembourg société à responsabilité limitée (a Luxembourg limited company) wholly owned by Camco Management Limited.

GCF Holding SCpA Holdings is proposed that the GCF participate in Camco REP SCSPs via a special purpose vehicle (GCF REP2 Holding SCpA). The GCF will act as a Luxembourg SCpA under the 1915 Law for which the GCF would be investor of record and no other limited partnership interests would be issued. GCF Proceeds would flow via

## B

### Portfolio HoldCo Structure

The dual-HoldCo structure is designed to address the twin aims of addressing the broader market demand for financing projects in renewables and building on the success of REPP Phase 1, while maintaining the strict separation of funds and revenues under the FAA. The dual HoldCo structure is a simple and cost-effective way of ensuring separation between investments (and resulting cash flows) at Host Countries (PCs) and GCF Proceeds. All proceeds from REPP Phase 1 are channelled via the AE; through GCF REPP Holdings SCps into Cameo REPP SCps will be available to Portfolio HoldCos.

The capital structure (i.e., same blended finance structure of junior equity, senior equity and senior debt) is broadly duplicated in both HoldCos (subscription is identical), as shown in the indicative table below. Senior debt is provided by a separate entity, which is not part of Cameo REPP SCps or Portfolio HoldCos. Investments and returns are made independently in each HoldCo.

It is possible that the amount of commitments of different classes could be different in each of Portfolio HoldCos 1 & 2 as well as Portfolio HoldCos 3 & 4. For example, a Junior Class Commitment to Portfolio HoldCo 1 S.A.r.l should not have a greater ratio of Junior Class Interests to Senior Class Interests as compared to Portfolio HoldCo 2 S.A.r.l., subject always to the restriction that Junior Class Interests may not (with effect from the date of subscription) be held in excess of their respective Commitments.

| Senior Equity | 100 | 40% | 50 | 40% | 50 | 40% |
