## Track Record
**Cancro is a climate and impact fund manager, leading the clean energy transition in emerging markets.**
- Having spent over thirty years in the renewable energy sector, we offer practical and value-added financing solutions to support projects that are aligned with our mission.
- As an independent entity of a private company, Cancro was formed in Nairobi in 1989, it is a regulated small Alternative Investment Fund Manager in the UK and is a Green Climate Fund Accredited Entity.

### Key Highlights
- Co-advisor of the USD200 million Green Africa Power (GAP) mandate, 2014–2017.
- Sole manager of the Renewable Energy Performance Platform (REPP) Phase I: GBP48 million first close; GBP35.6 million second close.
- Sponsor and manager of Spark Energy Services, a USD50 million, commercial and industrial energy efficiency fund in East Africa, which is now closed to new investments.
- Currently fundraising for Transforming Island Development for Electrification and Sustainability (TIDES), a USD120 million renewable energy and energy access facility for Pacific Islands.

### Projects
Over a 30-year track record, Cancro impacts include:
- **60+ projects completed.**
- **USD50 million committed with new and improved electricity capacity.**
- **600 MW installed capacity realized;** and
- **USD960 million in third party funding mobilized.**

### Experience
Cancro has a long-standing presence in the region, with offices in west, east and southern Africa:
- Detailed local knowledge from the ground up, combined with international reach.
- Extensive experience in project development, technical assistance, equity, debt, monitoring & evaluation, asset management and exit.
- Expertise across a range of key disciplines including policy regulation, market and project development, technical assistance, equity, debt, monitoring & evaluation, asset management and exit.
- Deep experience of financing small-scale renewables in Sub-Saharan Africa.

## Flow of Funds
### Disbursements
The GCF will commit USD50 million with GCF Proceeds provided to Cancro Management Limited as AE in six annual disbursements, in accordance with Annex 14 (Term Sheet).
- The GCF will pay fees to the AE in equal installments in accordance with Annex 14 (Term Sheet).
- The proceeds from the sale of REPP SC53 will be transferred to the AA and then transferred from the GCF Account to a dedicated account in the name of GCF REPP Holdings SC53.
- The GCF Proceeds will be transferred contemporaneously as a capital contribution to Cancro REPP SC53 as set out in the Subscription and Partnership Agreement.

B.5 Justification for GCF funding request

The proposed capital structure for REPP 2 is outlined in the table below.

| Instrument | Description | Final close (USD million) | Total |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Senior debt* | Impact and capital preservation with debt service | 100 | 100 |
| Equity | Impact and equity risk / return | 100 | 100 |
| Junior equity (concessional equity) | Impact and mobilisation of third-party funds | 100 | 100 |

The capital structure for REPP 2 is designed to help address key market failures for smaller-scale renewables across sub-Saharan Africa, including:

- A lack of early-stage or development funding for smaller-scale renewables; and
- A limited risk appetite from both public and private funders for early-stage and smaller-scale projects.

Approved GCF funding programmes for climate mitigation through renewables are typically focused on larger-scale renewable energy projects (Annex 23). This is also the case with REPP 2, which has a focus on funds particularly for smaller project sizes (Annex 23). There is also relatively limited geographic overlap between the capital structure of REPP 2 and programmes where funding by the GCF for climate mitigation funding from GCF for REPP 2 will be strongly complementary to existing funded activities.

In terms of climate adaptation, the GCF recognises the urgent and immediate needs of vulnerable communities in particular least developed countries (LDCs), small island developing States (SIDS) and African States* and has recently reaffirmed this decision. Despite this, the scale and scope of funding from the GCF for climate adaptation is still limited. The GCF’s 2021-25 strategy sets a target to increase its share of total approved funding within the GCF portfolio, with cross-cutting programmes making up a further 13%. Of the energy programmes reviewed in Annex 23, only two are cross-cutting programmes involving renewable energy and climate adaptation. These are consistent with the GCF’s 2021-25 strategy priorities and will make a materially positive impact to build climate resilience across the region.

There is no lack of financing for large-scale, commercially viable renewable energy projects across sub-Saharan Africa. However, there is an increasing need in the near-term picture for smaller-scale projects as well as nascent renewable energy markets in LDCs and middle-income countries where funding gaps exist. The same analysis highlights the paucity of funding available for early-stage growth capital of off-


* Terms dependent on current market conditions

* http://www.greenclimatefund.org/en/annexes/annex-23
* https://www.greenclimatefund.org/en/publication/data-browser/survived-pool-of-financing (last accessed 06 September 2022).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 41

## Platform financing instruments

REPP 2 is a tiered fund facility featuring a junior equity tranche, equity and debt. The proposed capital structure for REPP 2 (and the GCF's position within it), along with the selection of financing instruments used to leverage the capital stock have been designed to create in additional sources of both public and private capital.

The junior equity tranche, which will act as a first loss buffer, is designed to deliver incrementally greater impact at lower cost than debt. The GCF's position within REPP 2 will be limited to 10% of the total available capital stock. The sizing (at least 30% of total available capital) and subordinated position of this tranche in the REPP 2 capital stack will provide protection for all investors in more senior tranches. It is proposed that the GCF junior equity investment be limited to other investors in the NCL portfolio (Portfolio HoldCo 1 S.a.r.l.). The junior equity tranche will target low, single-digit returns in the base case.


---
**Page 46**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 42

The senior equity tranche is designed to provide more commercially oriented equity returns. Camco targets 40% of the total capital requirement in the form of senior equity from a combination of private investors, international financial institutions (IFIs) and development finance institutions (DFIs), all of whom are committed to providing long-term financing through the protection of returns provided by the junior equity tranche.

The senior debt tranche will employ maximum credit enhancement from the equity tranches. The senior debt tranche is expected to be financed by commercial debt providers.


## 3. Level of concessionality

The GCF’s strategic objective is to catalyse climate finance for mitigation and adaptation which is new, additional (in the sense that a project or programme would not have occurred in the absence of GCF funding), adequate and predictable.* Accordingly, REPP 2 will ensure that its investments are additional and concessional. The level of concessionality will be determined by the risk appetite of recipient countries.

In financing smaller-scale renewable energy projects and businesses, REPP 2 recognises the need to strike a balance between the need for concessionality, the levels of real and perceived risk and the desire to grow a sustainable market for renewables. In the African context, these risks are higher, given that the market is still in its infancy and there may be limited experience with renewable energy projects. REPP 2 will focus on the sector where balance of risk and return provided needs to match the risk appetite and requirements for all small-scale renewable energy businesses in Africa. LDCs struggle to attract funding in general, and particularly private and commercial funding. The fact that barriers and significant funding gaps persist is also a reflection of the need to provide additional concessional finance (see section B 2(a) and the need to develop a launched capital structure for REPP with a layer of junior equity senior equity and notes layers to attract private and commercial investors as much as possible, given limited experience in this sector.

At the level of the underlying assets funded by REPP 2, and in providing gap financing to smaller-scale grid-connected projects, REPP 2 will help developers and sponsors of these projects to overcome a range of barriers that are preventing them from accessing private and commercial funding. These include the commercially viable. Traditional investors, such as banks and utilities, often consider small-scale projects to be too risky and/or not sufficiently profitable. As a result, they tend to focus on larger projects. Private sector investors in the sector typically require minimum ticket sizes of USD10-20 million, which is often larger than the financing needed by small-scale projects. The economics of small-scale projects are often marginal, and the limited capacity of these projects means that they have a very low potential for revenue generation or profit generation limited by the limited installed capacity of the plant. For these reasons, REPP 2 will focus on closing this gap with concessional finance, with a risk-adjusted return expectation that is lower than what is typically required by the market.

To address these challenges, REPP offers flexible financing with an adjusted hurdle rate that is lower when compared to private and commercial investors. This helps to overcome the barriers to form a sustainable renewable energy projects in Sub-Saharan Africa and promote the development of these projects in the region.

Target for leveraging additional private and commercial capital at the level of the underlying assets funded by REPP 2 are also commensurate to the level of appetite from such investors. The target of leveraging USD383 million in private and commercial funding in the underlying investments represents 50% of the required total investment, which is a reasonable amount given the limited experience with renewable energy projects.


---
**Page 47**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 43

of REPP Phase II and it set conservatively in that the proportion of third party funding from private and commercial sources is no higher than that already achieved in REPP Phase I. While the AE is motivated to increase this proportion over time, the target is set conservatively in recognition that the risk appetite from private investors is likely to be lower for projects at an earlier stage of development than those which have been the investment.

In this context, GCF funding is highly catalytic, in attracting the exposed/leveraged co-financing targets and commercial investors and in leveraging additional co-financing at the level of the underlying investments.

REPP 2 acknowledges that it cannot price on risk for a purely commercial basis. If commercially viable projects had a ready supply of commercial funds already existed, the funding gaps identified in Section B(2)a) and Annex 23 would not exist and there would be no need for a facility such as REPP 2. The learnings from REPP 1 are that many renewable energy projects in Host Countries are commercially viable but because these renewable energy businesses struggle to access private capital on terms which leave their projects commercially viable and that public financing in this space is still required, in this context, funding for REPP 2 is needed.

The GCF junior equity facility is designed to provide a source of concessional funding to support the sectors in Host Countries. If REPP 2 adopted a purely concessional approach to pricing, the effect would be to negatively disrupt market dynamics and distort private sector investment decisions. A purely concessional pricing approach would lack financial rigour and have the unintended consequence of crowding out private sector funding. The approach to pricing is therefore designed to strike an appropriate balance between the need for a source of funding at levels of concessionality, in order to build the market and crowd in private sector funding, and the need for rigour and more commercial pricing approaches, where the market, investor appetite and project economics allow for it.

The concessionality of the GCF junior equity funding is defined to protect other more senior investors in the deal. The structure of the junior equity facility is designed to provide a source of funding at levels of concessionality (catalysing) other private and commercial investors. The junior equity tranches in Portfolio HoldCo1 S.a.r.l and Portfolio HoldCo2 5.s.a.rl (sized and priced identically) are designed to provide first loss protection to other investors in the deal, with a return on investment that is consistent with market pricing for similar structures and pricing of the junior equity tranche is met while also facilitating targeted returns for the equity providers. For senior debt providers, the structure and pricing of the capital stock also supports current market pricing.

### 4. Risk-sharing

The proposal for GCF and other public funding to private funding sources targeted for the REPP 2 fundraise (at a T:14 ratio) is designed to provide scale of financing that Cenmor expects can be mobilised into this sector, for the activities proposed in this Programme.

For the GCF, where an investment in the junior equity tranche is proposed, risk will be shared pari passu with other investors.

B.6. Exit strategy

# 1. Overall exit strategy

REPP 2 is a fixed-term (20-year) fund. The limited partners of Camco REPP SCSp may agree to extend the objects of the REPP 2 as further set out in Annex 14 (Term Sheet).

Junior debt will mature at the end of the 20-year term of REPP 2. Senior equity investors will earn a targeted return in the base case. Senior debt will be repaid in full (principal plus accrued interest) over a weighted average tenor of 7 years. The Subscription and Participation Agreement will, on customary terms, provide for the redemption of the senior equity investment by way of redemption or transfer for value by the limited member. The dissolution mechanisms for GCF REPP Holdings and Camco REPP SCSp are set out in Annexes 15 (Dissolution) and 16 (Dissolution), respectively.

Following the completion of the activities of REPP 2 (including the dissolution of the Portfolio HoldCo), the remaining assets will be sold, subject to any material warranthand the completion of an exit strategy. The final distribution will be made by Camco REPP SCSp as a General Partner to a Subsidiary Acquisition Holding Entity pursuant to a Subsidiary Agreement. The dissolution of GCF REPP Holdings will be made by Camco REPP SCSp as general partner of Camco REPP SCSp to the limited partners of Camco REPP SCSp for dissolution. In the event that the exit strategy is not completed within 12 months after the date on which the fund is cut out in due incorporation. Pursuant to, GCF REPP Holdings will be dissolved in a similar manner upon the satisfaction of all of the obligations of the Holdings GP under the Holdings GPA.

For equity investments, senior equity investors will earn a targeted return (principal plus accrued interest) over the loan tenor. Where possible, REPP 2 will seek to reduce or exit its debt investments through refinancing opportunities, either for individual transactions or at the portfolio level.

The objective of the active management of REPP 2 is to ensure that principal repayment plus returns over the targeted investment period. Where possible and profitable, REPP will seek an early exit from equity investments through new fundraising rounds and/or sell-off down existing shareholding to new investors.

The portfolio of active transactions for REPP 2 will be actively managed in order ensure orderly repayment of debt with positive returns and to proactively identify and execute opportunities for new investment, refinancing or sale.


# 2. Pricing approach

From a pricing perspective, the REPP 2 approach ensures an appropriate blend of concessionality and commerciality. This approach is designed to avoid market distortions and seeks to minimize market distortion as far as possible by crowding in other sources of private public funding rather than crowding out other funding through a pricing approach which is too concessional.

Where debt and equity investments are priced on a more concessional basis, the goal will be explicit to incentivize the development of projects that have a high likelihood of sustainability.

Where REPP 2 makes equity investments in off-grid companies, opportunities for co-investment and sell-down exist as these businesses grow (later fundraises) – which may represent opportunities for REPP 2 to acquire an interest at a later stage.


---
**Page 49**
---

Where business or project economics allow, REPP 3 will invest on purely commercial terms. This approach ensures minimum market distortion, maximises pricing rigor in line with market benchmarks and ensures that funds can be returned or repaid to REPP 2 and can achieve return for risk.

### Paradigm shift

REPP 2 helps to innovate and transform the market for small-scale renewable energy in each of the Host Countries by promoting skills and technology transfer, development of an active market with direct private sector participation are all permanent. These elements achieve the targeted paradigm shift potential.

With the expectation of SHS systems, all technologies will have a lifetime over 20 years, therefore impacting cost per kilowatt-hour (kWh) significantly lower than current grid electricity tariffs. This is achieved through the business model, offering affordable energy to vulnerable communities, and hence providing long-term service beyond technical lifetime of the system. Monitoring and verification of the effectiveness of the projects is conducted by independent third parties. The results will be reported to its stakeholders, through annual reports on its key KPIs, which are published on the REPP 2 website. Through organic growth of host country financial markets and with REPP 2 demonstration effect it is expected that other developers and funds we adopt a similar approach.

A technical assistance component of REPP 2 will be funded separately to the Programme on a grant-funded basis through the Technical Assistance Facility (to be established as a separate legal entity). Until the establishment of this facility, a small amount of REPP 2 funding will be allocated to support market development, skills and capacity building and technology transfer.10

REPP 2 funding for skills development and capacity building within Host Countries as well as for the small-scale technical assistance component:

- Build the capacity, skills and experience of renewable energy project developers, both local and international;
- Contribute to a stronger enabling environment for renewables through building institutional and policy frameworks; and
- Ensure business model innovation and technology transfer.

These activities will be funded out of normal operating costs of REPP 2 (with contributions from all investors in the Fund) based on that budget that is expected to be approved by the general partner of Camco REPP SICP.

## 2. Mitigation outcomes

### Outcome 1: Reduced GHG emissions from renewable energy installations

The mitigation targets will be estimated using a technology split based on REPP 2’s transaction pipeline, and the total annual electricity generation estimate (UNFCCC-approved grid emission factors and average technology lifetimes). The energy baseline is the total annual electrical generation or renewable energy generation avoided by the Programme. The four categories of renewable energy are: AMS-L – Hydroelectricity generation (version 18), AMS-L – Grid-connected renewable electricity generation (version 18), and AMS-L – Electrification of rural communities using renewable energy (version 4). All mitigation targets will be aligned with host-country targets and priorities.

All figures shown here reflect targets for the full REPP 2 Programme, not merely the portion allocable to GCF funding. *Life cycle assumptions: Solar PV mini-grids (SHS) = 15 years; solar PV systems (SHS) = 20 years; solar PV ground-mounted = 25 years; hydropower = 25 years; solar PV mini-grids (SHS) = 5 years.

3. Adaptation outcomes

Outcome 2: Improved climate resilience of energy infrastructure

The value of the energy infrastructure made resilient is estimated based on the estimated value of the pipeline of projects in countries in which the energy system is currently vulnerable due to (1) a reliance on hydroelectric power, which are prone to drought and 2) grid vulnerability due to weak transmission corridors and poor grid maintenance and operation, leading to frequent outages and grid instability. This methodology is consistent with GCF core indicator 3 value of physical assets made more resilient to the effects of climate change.

Currently, in average over 20% of all REPP 2 on-grid investments are anticipated to assist in building resilience of energy infrastructure. Resulting to USD 37 million of assets made more climate-resilient. Please see table below for details.

Outcome 3: Improved climate resilience of vulnerable people and communities

Improved climate resilience of vulnerable people and communities will be achieved through the provision of access to clean energy, which improves the ability of off-grid communities to respond to climate impacts and build their own resilience.

The number of people who will adopt improved livelihood options has been estimated with reference to the REPP Phase 1 experience, specifically relating to the number of productive uses of energy (PUE) activities and the number of customers that will be provided with critical services. For example, in Mozambique it is estimated that 20% of REPP 2 off-grid customers will be microbusinesses and approximately half of these microbusinesses are anticipated to improve their livelihoods through access to clean energy. This includes improved cooking, lighting, cold storage, refrigeration, electric milling, improved access to markets through mobility etc. Similarly based on REPP Phase 1 data it is estimated that 1% of original customers will be providers of critical services e.g., clinics and hospitals, schools, drinking water stations, cell phone towers etc.

4. Enabling outcomes

Outcome 4: Increased private sector investment

Finance mobilised for renewable energy generation benefits local developers, builds a stronger local market for smaller-scale renewables and helps to build gender equity.

REPP 2 will work to mobilise the private and public finance necessary to give a robust and viable market for renewable energy in Africa. REPP 2 will also seek to overcome historic barriers to finance and demonstrate the bankability of projects to crowd-in other investors. In addition, REPP 2 will facilitate knowledge sharing between developers and financial institutions through capacity building workshops and support successful project implementation. REPP 2 is anticipated to mobilise USD786 million additional finance towards climate-smart renewable energy infrastructure.

USD786 million - of which USD305 million is expected to assist the USD10 billion funding gap facing female entrepreneurs in Africa. REPP 2 will aim to dedicate a portion of its funding to renewable energy companies in line with 2X Challenge's Gender Lens Investment (2X GLI) criteria. This means the company must:

- be founded by a woman or women or be majority-owned by women; and / or
- have at least 30% women in senior management; and / or
- provide products, services or solutions that benefit women; and / or
- have a workforce that is at least 30% women, together with a progressive human resource policy.

# Outcome 5: Contributing to stronger technology deployment, innovation and market transformation

## REPP 2 will contribute to stronger enabling market environment for renewables through building capacity and financing a stronger longer life and faster technology deployment, innovation and market development in Host Countries (HCs) and across the region.

### Core Indicator 6: REPP 2 will contribute to more and more rapid technology deployment by providing targeted financial support at critical stages of the asset lifecycle, including project preparation, feasibility studies, construction stages of the assets. REPP 2 will also finance the deployment of renewable energy technology in nascent markets, often financing first-of-a-kind transactions and business models.

### Core Indicator 7: REPP 2 will stimulate sectoral development that would otherwise lag or not occur (additional indicators are provided for this indicator). REPP 2 will provide targeted financial support to small and medium-sized renewables in Host Countries and across the region (transformations). REPP 2 will take a multi-pronged approach to achieve this, including: (i) providing project preparation grants; (ii) hosting Country governments and regulators to help to develop a viable renewable energy sector, and facilitating access to finance for small-scale renewables projects in Host Countries and across the region; and (iii) supporting the development of local developers and financing institutions that can support small and medium-sized renewables projects. REPP 2 will help to build skills and establish disciplines which underpin a commercially-viable market for smaller-scale renewables through its support for local developers and local financing institutions.

### Core Indicator 8: REPP 2 builds on the early successes of REPP Phase I by mobilising finance from public, private and other commercial sources, using its blended finance approach. REPP 2 aims to deliver a paradigm shift for the small-scale renewables sector within Host Countries and across the region as a whole, with demonstration potential more broadly across emerging markets.

## D

### Repowering support for renewables technologies, services and business models in nascent markets provides an opportunity to demonstrate technical and commercial viability, implement new regulatory frameworks and crowd-in public and private finance.

In the case of REPP, it builds on existing successes achieved by REPP Phase I, such as in Burundi, where REPP Phase 1 support for the first solar PV IPP in the country demonstrated the potential of renewables to provide reliable power at competitive costs and expand its market for renewable energy IPPs. Another example can be seen from Sierra Leone, where REPP-Phase-I investment provides electricity access and access to PUC equipment through an innovative financing model that allows for a phased approach to achieving incremental increases in service to low income customers.

### The design of the REPP 2 support facility ensures that GCF and other public sector funds can be rapidly mobilized. In addition, the blended finance structure of REPP 2 is designed to de-risk investments. With an expected aggregate portfolio loss of 20%, the junior equity provides protection against losses in the event of a default by the senior debt providers, including private debt providers, enjoy most risk-reduction through the blended finance structure.

### This platform for scale, replicability and sustainability at the project and investment level

#### For REPP 2 investments in off-grid companies help to expand to nascent markets, achieve growth, commercialization and long-term sustainability.
- For grid connected projects, funding and support from REPP 2 supports the development and implementation of technically- and commercially viable projects which operate for design lifetimes of 25 years or more. The REPP 2 investment model is designed to be flexible in terms of how funding combined with the knowledge, skills and technology transfer to local developers, funders, institutions and governments can help scale-up the enabling environment for small-scale renewables based on commercial best practice.
- At a community level, REPP 2 especially for off-grid electrification builds climate resilience for vulnerable communities and groups.

#### By providing funding at scales for both off-grid and grid-connected renewables, REPP 2 helps to reduce poverty and improve access to energy services in rural areas. This can have significant benefits for people and communities in rural areas, as well as improve the climate resilience of energy infrastructure. These represent durable and significant contributions to climate-resilient development pathways which are consistent with relevant national climate change adaptation strategies and plans.

D.3. Sustainable development

# 1. Sustainable Development Goals

REPP 2 contributes to the following SDGs:

- **SDG 1: No Poverty;**
- **SDG 5: Gender Equality;**
- **SDG 7: Affordable Clean Energy;**
- **SDG 8: Decent Work and Economic Growth;**
- **SDG 11: Sustainable Cities and Communities**
- **SDG 13 Climate Action**
- **SDG 14 Life Below Water for the Global Goals**

The ultimate beneficiaries of REPP 2 funding for off-grid projects are the communities and businesses who will gain often first-time access to a clean, reliable and affordable source of electricity, which can contribute significantly towards achieving SDGs. In addition, REPP 2 interconnected projects supported by REPP 2 will provide clean electricity to the grid that will contribute to national GHG emission reductions helping to build resilience against climate change.

# 2. Environmental co-benefits (co-benefit 4)

The potential environmental and social impacts of all projects will be managed according to the REPP 2 Environmental and Social Management Framework as approved by the general partner of Cemco Renewable SCIs) and the Environmental and Social Management (ESM) Framework (Annex 6). Both the Policy and Procedures Manual and the ESM Framework are based on the IFC Performance Standards. A specific environmental and social management system (ESMS) will be established to mitigate and manage any negative impacts. REPP 2 capacity building activities and early-stage funding will improve environmental and social performance of projects. The ESMS will ensure that all projects meet or exceed the standards as measured through the establishment and implementation of high quality ESMs and will lead to benefits such as improved air quality, reduced water pollution, reduced impact to biodiversity, soil and water resources. In addition to GHG mitigation, additional co-benefits, such as improved ambient air quality are expected to occur. However, these are not quantified.

# 3. Social co-benefits including health co-benefit

At the household level, the small renewable energy projects that REPP 2 will offer multiple environmental and social benefits (co-benefit 4). These include improved quality of life through communities and critical services connected to electricity (co-benefit 3). Co-benefits include improved access to lighting, heating, cooling, cooking, water supply, sanitation, and mobile phone charging. This will also create new job opportunities. These co-benefits will allow further socioeconomic development of Host Countries and their off-grid communities. There are particular co-benefits for women and girls, where greater energy access allows them more time to pursue education and other activities that improve their quality of life. Access to renewable energy through REPP 2 will act as a driver for long-term economic growth in local communities, increasing their resilience to climate change. These co-benefits will contribute towards the achievement of SDG 1.


---
**Page 58**
---

# 4. Economic co-benefits

Renewable energy projects have the potential to create employment opportunities, both during development and operation, across numerous skill sets, from engineering and construction to sales and customer services (co-benefits 1). As a result, first-time access to energy also generates economic co-benefits through new opportunities for social and economic development through extended working hours and greater efficiency in production. Renewable energy projects can also provide income-generating activities that provide decent work, support labour rights, and improve skills in line with the REPP 2 Environmental and Social Management Framework and the ESMA 3000 framework to support the achievement of SDG 8.

# 5. Gender-specific drivers for economic co-benefits (co-benefit 1)

The positive impacts identified above will specifically benefit women, since across rural communities in Africa in particular women and girls are the primary energy users in the household, responsible for sourcing fuel, paying for it and managing its use. REPP 2 will be committed to building capacity on gender equality in line with the ESMA 3000 framework. This will include training of staff and partners in gender equality issues in establishing and implementing gender action plans. All companies are required to establish action plans addressing gender equality at all levels, including at the implementation level.

In addition, a target of 30% of REPP 2 funding will be directed to assist the USD42 billion funding gap female energy users face in Africa. This will help ensure that women and girls benefit from the co-benefits of renewable energy projects in line with the ESMA 3000 framework to support the achievement of SDG 5 – Gender Equality.

# D. Needs of recipient

## 6. Vulnerability of the country and beneficiary groups

Country-specific climate hazards, vulnerabilities, and impacts include:1)

- **Cameroon**: Hazards include drought, heavy rainfall events, flooding, heavy winds, high temperatures, and intense cyclones in the south-west and east, and a more intense wet season elsewhere. The energy sector is extremely vulnerable to climate change due to its reliance on hydroelectric power for thermal and industrial cooling, as well as drought, and sea-level rise. Rural electricity access is low (25% and 26%) of population lives in poverty making them especially vulnerable to climate impacts.
- **DRC**: Hazards include heavy rainfall events, floods, heatwaves, and seasonal droughts. Reliance on the use of traditional biomass significantly increases the climate vulnerability of the Population Rural electricity access is low (35% and 30%) of population lives in poverty making them extremely vulnerable to climate impacts.
- **Madagascar**: Hazards include intensification of cyclones and associated flooding, sea-level rise, intensification of droughts in the south and west, and a more intense wet season elsewhere. The energy sector is extremely vulnerable to climate change due to its reliance on hydroelectric power for thermal and industrial cooling, as well as drought, and sea-level rise. Rural electricity access is low (15% and 20%) of population lives in poverty making them especially vulnerable to climate impacts.
- **Mozambique**: Hazards include floods, strong winds, intense rainfall, rivers and flash floods and tropical cyclones. Vulnerabilities include poverty, rapid population growth, dependence on the agricultural sector reliance on rainfed agriculture and biomass as well as lack of financing and weak institutions.

# Needs for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity

REPP 2 contributes to the development of an enabling environment in Host Countries by supporting technology transfer and capacity development of local developers. Through its investments, which are often focused on renewable energy projects, REPP 2 also supports the development of a conducive policy environment by engaging with government stakeholders—directly or through its investors—to discuss the regulatory frameworks and standard project documentation, sharing a financial’s perspective on what is required to ensure that projects can be developed in an efficient manner. REPP 2 will also continue to work closely with private sector investors to fund the renewable energy priorities which are key to target country climate change adaptation strategies. In addition, REPP 2 will support the development of a regulatory framework and regulatory footprints for mini-grids in Nigeria to further strengthen the successful and timely implementation of its investment programme. REPP 2 will also continue to support the Government of Niger with its renewable energy generation programme which would successfully attract private investment; supporting the Government of Niger in strengthening public sector engagement in renewable energy development. As it further develops its focus on renewable energy, REPP 2 will also continue to engage with other countries in Africa that are going through the GCF accreditation process (such assistance is already being discussed in Nigeria and Liberia).

## Country ownership

REPP 2 will place a strong focus on ensuring country ownership of its activities. While it will apply a bottom-up approach to its operations, driven by demand from renewable energy project developers and companies, the portfolio will be aligned with national policy priorities and climate strategies. REPP 2 will ensure that all investments are aligned with national policies. The same approach will be critical to ensuring country ownership of REPP 2.

## Alignment with and contribution to national priorities and climate strategies

In 2020, Cameco undertook an analysis of the key policies in all REPP Phase 1 countries of operation and assessed the extent to which the portfolio was aligned with key national policy targets and priorities. The assessment showed that REPP 2 is well-aligned with national policy objectives in all countries where it operates. This alignment will ensure that REPP 2’s technology focus and support for the increase of renewable energy generation capacity and diversification of energy systems is strongly aligned with all Host Country NDCs. Most of these countries also consider renewable energy development as a strong adaptation measure through the improvement of access to electricity, especially in rural areas and smallholder households, with particular attention given to women and vulnerable groups—the resilience of local populations.

Through its investments, REPP 2 will support the numerous national priorities outlined below: A more detailed description is available on the website.

- Reduce emissions by 50% by 2030 through increased renewable energy capacity and expanded energy access (conditional: Updated NDC)
- Reduce energy sector’s vulnerability through focus on small-scale and distributed generation (National Climate Change Adaptation Plan)

## Engagement with NDAs and other stakeholders
In early 2011, Camco began the process of obtaining no objection letters (NOLs) from the respective National Designated Authority (NDA) in most of the countries that will be supported through REPP 2 GCF's contribution. The NDA is responsible for ensuring that the proposed activities and operational model are explained. The REPP 2 draft Concept Note was shared with key stakeholders on February 10, 2011. Following this sharing, several questions and concerns were raised, which feedback integrated into the final version of the Concept Note and this Funding Proposal. The Funding Proposal has also been shared and discussed with the NDAs. The ESMF is shared in the annexes.

As part of the engagement process, Camco consulted respective NDAs and other relevant sectoral stakeholders, particularly ministries of energy and rural electrification agencies, virtually and/or in person (in-country) meetings were held with the following countries: Benin, Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Ethiopia, Ghana, Liberia, Nigeria, Sierra Leone and Zambia, as well as several sectoral stakeholders). The aim of these consultations was to ensure that the proposed activities and operational model are explained. These consultations took place during the approval process of this Funding proposal and throughout the REPP 2 operational timeframe, which will include annual progress updates and ad hoc engagement as required to discuss specific implementation issues. In addition, Camco has engaged with relevant sectoral stakeholders in each country before initial GCF Board approval, provided them an NOL, and the addition is approved by the GCF Board. As part of this ongoing engagement, Camco has engaged with multiple private sector stakeholders and civil society representatives.

Country ownership of REPP 2 once operational, will be ensured through regular dialogue and long-term relationships established with national governments and other relevant national stakeholders (identified by the NDA). This will include:
- Annual updates to NDAs and sectoral stakeholders on the progress of REPP 2 activities and broader discussions.
- Ad hoc engagement with relevant government agencies, management units of sector support programs, and/or non-governmental institutions and civil society organizations to ensure alignment with national policies and strategies, including those related to EAS and gender-mainstreaming policies; and
- Ad hoc engagement as part of investment due diligence (IDG) process.

Once the Programme is operational, REPP 2 will promote its activities internationally and in Host Countries through industry associations and on-the-ground network and partners to ensure local companies in those markets are aware of it and have the opportunity to benefit from the REPP 2 offering.

## 3. Suitability of the Accredited Entity

As a climate and impact fund manager, Camco seeks to deliver on its vision for "A brighter future, today," by supporting the clean energy transition in emerging markets. With local offices and staff in West, East and South Africa, as well as Europe and Asia Pacific, Camco has over 20 years of experience to deliver value as both the REPP Phase 1 manager and a GCF Accredited Entity.

As the REPP Phase 1 manager, Camco has been integral to the programme's design and implementation since its inception in 2013. As an accredited entity under the GCF, Camco will continue to perform the REPP Phase 1 management role separately from delivering its obligations as a GCF Accredited Entity. From a governance perspective, this separation of roles and responsibilities is important to maintain independence and focus on delivery.

Camco (as a GCF Accredited Entity), REPP 2, and the GCF, leveraging their respective skills, experience and resources, will work together to deliver the REPP 2 programme in an integrated manner. Camco, which is a signatory to the UN Global Compact, recognizes its responsibility for opportunity to support both the Paris climate targets and the Sustainable Development Goals. Both remain core objectives of the GCF.

## D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness

### 1. Rationale for a blended finance approach

The supply of private (and public) capital is scarce for smaller-scale renewables across sub-Saharan Africa, and the limited risk appetite of (particularly) private sources of funding exacerbates this situation. In order to provide sufficient scale and access to energy services with climate change impacts, the finance mobilization must expand beyond the single grant-based donor approach of REPP Phase 1. The blended finance approach is therefore necessary for REPP 2, as it provides a mechanism necessary to finance projects at scale and assist the targeted countries in achieving their climate goals.

### 2. Adequacy and reasonableness

As outlined in detail in Section B.5, the proposed capital structure for REPP 2, and the GCF's position within it, is designed to leverage both public sources of both public and private capital. The GCF investment in the junior equity tranche would also be shared by another investor with these investments mobilizing additional public and private investment in more senior equity and debt tranches.

REPP 2 will seek to raise funds on a risk-sharing commercial basis. Since small-scale developers often struggle to access private capital on terms that leave their projects commercially viable, public financing in this space is still required. In this context, funding for REPP 2 will contribute to the continued development of the market and the ability of small-scale developers to access finance for the delivery of climate mitigation and adaptation impacts in Host Countries.

On the other hand, if REPP 2 adopted a purely concessional approach to pricing, it may have the unintended consequence that it would not be able to compete with private sector funding in Host Countries outcompeting private sector funding. REPP 2's approach to concessionality thus will strive to achieve a considered balance between the need for concessionality, in order to build the market and crowd in private sector funding, and


---
**Page 64**
---

The need for more flexible and more commercial funding approaches, where the market, investor appetite and policies allow it.

# Efficiency and effectiveness

At the fund level, GCF’s funding would raise an additional USD200 million from public and private sources (USD150 million in the junior tranche of the capital stock. Expressed as a ratio of funding expected to be mobilised in more senior tranches (USD150 million), this would represent a funding mobilisation ratio of 3:1. Including funding expected to be mobilised into the junior equity tranche alongside the GCF, this ratio would be even higher.

At the level of investments planned to be made by REPP 2, the GCF investment would be expected to mobilise total finance (total project costs less REPP2 investments) in excess of USD786 million. This would represent a private sector leverage ratio of approximately 3:1.

REPP will aim to mobilise an additional US$500 million from private investors into both the equity and senior debt tranches. This would represent a private sector leverage ratio of 1:1. The analysis presented in Section 2.4 shows that REPP 2 is likely to be more successful than REPP 1 at mobilising private capital, in particular, as key barriers for the small-scale renewable energy sector in sub-Saharan Africa. Given the Small-Scale Renewable Energy Fund (SSEF) and REPP 2 are complementary initiatives, the combined funding quantum are lower and the potential for returns relative to the level of costs are constrained, it appears unrealistic to target a significantly higher private sector leverage ratio.

The GCF is committed to catalysing climate finance for mitigation and adaptation which is new, additional, adequate and predictable. This commitment is reflected in the REPP 2 indicative pipeline, which shows total estimated project cost (including fixed financing costs per MW of installed capacity), per technology. In general, cost efficiencies for small-scale grid-connected projects and off-grid electrification programmes will be lower than global benchmarks for large-scale projects. The higher costs are due to a number of factors including the need to supply chain and logistics, when purchasing equipment in smaller quantities and needing to maintain operations and maintenance (O&M) services at a local level. The cost of financing small-scale renewables also reflects the higher cost of capital for such businesses and projects, another financing barrier which REPP 2 aims to address. Total installed cost estimates from the REPP 2 indicative pipeline and relevant benchmarks are below:

| Offshore wind | 2.934 | 1.325 | IRENA, 2022 |
| Hydropower | 590 | 2.135 | IRENA, 2022* (not restricted to small scale hydropower) |
| Solar mini-grids | 4.908 | 3.659 | ESMAP, 2022** (the ESMap benchmark covers capital costs only) |
| Solar home systems | 3.174 | 1600 | IRENA, 2016* |

### Medium

**E7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)**

### 1. Monitoring and reporting

Monitoring and reporting will be conducted in accordance with industry best practice, following the CDM standards mentioned above and the guidance from Global GHG Accounting and Reporting Standard for the Financial Industry. The monitoring methodology to estimate ex-ante and to calculate ex-post carbon emission reductions is as per section E5.

In addition to the arrangements laid out in the Accreditation Master Agreement and the Funded Facility Agreement between Camco and the GCF, the implementation of REPP 2 will involve:

- Monitoring of environmental performance indicators (EPIs) as per section E5;
- Monitoring of key environmental and social (EAS) indicators for project-level compliance with the REPP 2 ESMS Framework, to be approved by the general partner of Camco REPP SCSp;
- Monitoring of portfolio companies' financial performance; and
- Periodic reporting and reporting of EPIs and EAS indicators.

REPP 2's monitoring and evaluation (M&E) arrangements are outlined in the REPP 2 Environmental and Social Policy and Procedures and the REPP 2 Operations Manual, to be approved by the general partner of Camco REPP SCSp. The M&E process will include:

- Training investees in REPP 2's M&E processes and requirements by the time of signing a Term Sheet;
- Executing an M&E plan for each investment; and
- Agreeing a monitoring plan with investees as part of their ESMS, which will be part of the obligations pursuant to the Support Agreement with each investee. This will detail the indicators each investee will monitor and report to REPP 2 on a quarterly basis;


---
**Page 85**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 81

- Quarterly monitoring of portfolio companies' financial performance.
- Collection of KPI and EAS data and supporting documents on a quarterly basis with each investee via the REPP 2 Impact Data Management System (presented in Annex 11B), to be approved by the general partner of Camoo REPP SCSPs. The investees will report on site-specific data, including financial performance, environmental impact, social impact, and country context for each investment. This allows aggregation of monitoring data and indicators by investor, country and technology.
- An annual investee review which is used to assess the fitness of investees ESMS implementation and to verify KPIs reported. This review is conducted by REPP 2 Manager.

REPP 2 will provide KPI updates to the AFIM, REPP 2 Investment Committee and REPP 2 Advisory Board on a quarterly basis and a full M&amp;R report in the annual M&amp;R report.

An important aspect of REPP 2's risk management process and impact verification will be the annual review of investments. The annual review will assess fitness of ESMS implementation and verify the reported impact indicators. The annual reviews will be based on document reviews (As an example, a review may include a comparison between actual performance against the baseline targets set during the due diligence process). The records will be provided by the investee company and reviewed by REPP managers impact team.
- If the review raises any discrepancies or material issues with respect to the ESMS implementation or reported impacts, a site visit will be conducted as an additional part of the annual review. The results of the annual review will confirm whether support can be continued in its current form, if modifications of the REPP 2 support are needed, or if withdrawal of REPP 2 support is required.

### Evaluation

REPP 2's evaluation process is outlined in the Independent Evaluation of Camoo Investment Mandates policy. The evaluation is an independent review of the performance and impact of each investment against the baseline targets set during due diligence (and any other relevant indicators). The evaluation will be conducted by an independent third party, with a final report to be submitted to the REPP 2 Manager (and AFIM) for review to ensure activities compliance with Camoo policies and procedures. An open tender procurement procedure will be followed to select the appropriate evaluator.

- **Independence:** Evaluations must be conducted independently from the delivery and management of financial assistance and project implementation.
- **Credibility:** Evaluations must be credible and based on reliable data and evidence. Evaluation reports should reflect consistency and dependability in data, findings and lessons learned, with reference to the quality of the procedures and analysis used to collect and interpret information.
- **Impartiality:** Evaluations must give a comprehensive and balanced presentation of the strengths and weaknesses of what is being evaluated. The evaluation process should reflect impartiality at all stages and consider the views of all stakeholders.


---
**Page 86**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE E2

- **Transparency:** Transparency involves clear communication on the criteria applied, the evaluation approach and methods, and the intended use of the findings. Documentation related to evaluations must be freely available, easily accessible and readable for transparency and legitimacy.
- **Integrity:** Evaluators must honour the dignity, well-being and self-worth of individuals and acknowledge the influence of culture within and across groups.
- **Gender equity:** Evaluations must assess whether – and if so, how - men and women are affected by changes to natural resource use and development.
- **Competencies and capacities:** Evaluations require a range of expertise that may be technical, environmental, cultural, ethical or within a social context. Evaluators must have sufficient experiences and skills in the appropriate fields.

### 3. Evaluation reporting of Camco (Accredited Entity) to GCF

Camco will provide the GCF with:
- Annual activity performance reports on the status of the Programme throughout the relevant reporting period;
- A mid-term evaluation report at the midpoint of the implementation period of the Programme; and
- A final evaluation report at the end of the implementation period of the Programme as per the funded activity agreement.

# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1. Risk Factors and Mitigation Measures

### Selected Risk Factor 1 Co-Financing Risk

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | High |

**Description**

The risk that an insufficient amount of co-financing is raised to meet GCF co-financing requirements or deliver the targeted impacts for REPP 2. There are additional risks that the terms on which co-financing can be secured, particularly in the current high-inflation, rising interest-rate environment, limit the potential for REPP 2 to be commercially viable and deliver targeted impacts and returns for investors.

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

- Fundraising efforts on behalf of REPP 2 are ongoing and at an advanced stage.
- In the event that the amount of co-financing raised is below the targeted level, the capital structure for REPP 2 is sufficiently flexible to ensure that all GCF requirements in terms of co-financing ratios will be met.
- The revised financial model incorporates an expectation of higher pricing and return expectations in light of current macro-economic trends.

### Selected Risk Factor 2 Investment Performance Risk

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

- Ongoing monitoring of asset performance and revenue-driven cash flows, including annual review cycle for investments, including site visits.

# Selected Risk Factor 9 Currency Risk

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Forex | High | Medium |

## Description

Where mismatches exist between revenues received by investors or borrowers in local currency and their hard-currency funding obligations, there is a risk that they will be unable to meet those obligations (in part or fully). This risk can be mitigated through the use of foreign exchange (FX) hedging. In case of failure to achieve targeted returns for its investors and in a worst-case scenario, financial losses at the portfolio level may result from FX mismatches between revenues denominated in local currency and liabilities denominated in hard currencies, especially in comparison to the lower revenues generated by similar renewable energy projects.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

- In general – and particularly for grid-connected projects – REPP 2 will invest and provide loans denominated in hard currency, effectively transferring currency risk to investors and borrowers. Where project agreements are denominated in local currency but the investor or borrower companies generate revenues in hard currencies, this risk can be passed on to the off-taker. Where the PPA is denominated in local currency and the investor or borrower companies generate revenues in local currency, currency risk lies with the investor/borrower.
- In rate cases where project economics allow, currency risk may be reduced through FX hedging, with either the investor or borrower company or a third party.
- REPP 2 will typically only consider offering investments in local currency where the investment or project is located in a country where hard-currency lending is not available (such as on-lending facilities via local financing partners).
- REPP 2 may also provide guarantees to investors and borrowers against FX risk for investors and a reduction in the costs of hedging for individual investors or borrowers through aggregation and scale.
- The measures can reduce the probability of occurrence of delays and losses.

# Selected Risk Factor 10 Currency Transfer and Inconvertibility Risk

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Forex | Medium | Medium |

## Description

In relation to currency risk, the additional risk that funds received by companies in local currency in a hard-currency environment may not be convertible into foreign currencies at any time or in an orderly manner. Delays in this process may result in losses for the investor or borrower due to currency mismatches between revenues and liabilities. For REPP 2, the result may be lower-than-expected returns and defaults in a worst-case scenario.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

- Secure government guarantees or assurance regarding access to foreign currency and transfer procedures in Host Countries.
- Where available, secure transfer and convertibility protection as part of a political risk insurance package.
- Work with local authorities (including central banks) to develop, implement and familiarise local staff procedures for the processing (preparation, approval, etc.) of currency conversion and transfer.

- Engagement and training provided to institutions in Host Countries responsible for the oversight and implementation of ESS. This includes training on the identification, assessment, mitigation and safeguarding policy requirements which are appropriate for the scale and risk associated with each facility.
- Early-stage development funding to prepare project level ESAs and ESMS
- Ongoing monitoring, reporting and evaluation on environmental and social risks during construction and operation in accordance with REPP 2 Environmental and Social Policy E. The measures described above can adequately minimise and manage environmental and social risks and the implementation of the ESMS will be monitored by the IFC.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

An Environmental and Social Policy and Procedures (ES&S Policy) and an Environmental and Social Management Framework (Framework) have been developed for REPP 2 to manage the environmental and social (E&S) impacts and risks of investments made by REPP 2. The ES&S Policy and Framework are in line with the GCF’s E&S policies and standards.

The Framework is presented in Annex 6.

### E&S risk and impacts of smaller renewable energy projects funded by REPP 2 are anticipated to be limited

The environmental and social risks expected to be low and contained within the footprint of the projects. The impacts should be largely reversible and easily mitigated through the mitigation measures implemented by investors, including those required under the ES&A/ESMS.

REPP 2 will not support investments if material negative E&S impacts cannot be satisfactorily mitigated.

### The E&S risk screening process for REPP 2 (elaborated in the Framework) is:

1. **Step 1:** The initial E&S risk screening occurs prior to investment approval and involves a Risk Assessment (RA) to determine whether an E&S category and eligibility of the project.
2. **Step 2:** An ES&A co-ordinating with the investor company takes place if the project is approved for further consideration and once the initial E&S risk screening has been completed to ensure the company understands E&S requirements and processes for REPP 2.
3. **Step 3:** Once the ES&A and ESMS have been finalised, REPP 2 will conduct a due diligence site visit to verify that the project meets all relevant criteria of the ES&A/ESMS. The findings from this assessment, potential or necessary resettlement, security risks, protected biodiversity impacts and human rights assessments, will be highlighted in the E&S Flag Report, and the extent of the risk will be verified during the due diligence site visit.
4. **Step 4:** Once the ES&A and ESMS have been finalised, REPP 2 will conduct a due diligence site visit to verify that all project activities align within their allocated category. All projects funded by REPP 2 will undertake a due diligence site visit to ensure alignment with the ES&A/ESMS. The assessment of resources, competence and aptitude for managing E&S risks in accordance with REPP 2 E&S standards.

REPP 2 is responsible for the implementation of the ES&S Policy and the Framework and shall apply an E&S risk categorisation to each potential investment. REPP 2 will have a dedicated impact team which will review each individual investment’s E&S risk, ensure the sufficient quality of the ES&A and the ESMS, assess the alignment with the ES&A/ESMS and determine whether further mitigation measures are required if required. The roles and responsibilities related to the implementation of the Framework are described in greater detail.

The monitoring of REPP 2 will involve:

- Monitoring of key performance indicators;
- Monitoring of key E&S indicators to ensure project-level compliance with the Framework;
- Periodic reporting; and
- Annual investors’ performance reviews to assess the fitness of its ESMS.


---
**Page 98**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 94

The monitoring and reporting obligations will be included in each Investment Agreement between REPP2 and the investee who will submit monitoring reports through an impact data management system on a quarterly basis. Reports will be reviewed by REPP2, and corrective action measures discussed with the investee.

In its capacity as an AE, Camco has been undertaking extensive stakeholder engagement with NDAs, energy ministries, agencies and regulators, as well as the private sector and relevant NGOs. This engagement is ongoing and will continue throughout the project lifecycle. The AE will be required to conduct appropriate site-based stakeholder consultations with communities and project beneficiaries in line with the Framework. Stakeholder engagement outcomes and strategy are further outlined in Section 4.

REPP2 will require that a grievance mechanism for stakeholders is established to receive and facilitate resolution of affected communities' concerns and grievances about a project's E&S performance. The preferred method of communication will be through an online portal, but other methods (e.g., phone calls, letters) are also preferred. The investee will provide guidelines on how grievances, claims or suggestions from the community can be submitted to the AE. These guidelines should include information for affected communities (e.g., members, service providers, and suppliers). In addition, Camco in its capacity as an AE, provides a whistleblower hotline and grievance form on its website that will allow submission of grievances in an anonymous manner.

REPP2 will recognize the critical importance of respecting the rights of Indigenous Peoples and of avoiding any adverse impacts on them. The Framework includes an Indigenous Peoples Policy (IPP) which will be applied to all projects where there is a significant risk of adverse impact on Indigenous Peoples' attachment or rights to the land. The IPP has been guided by the UN Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous, IFC E&S Performance Standard 6 and the World Bank’s Green Climate Fund Indigenous People Policy and is presented in Annex 6. As per the IPP, a stakeholder engagement process with Indigenous Peoples, and in the case of negative impacts the principle of free, prior and informed consent will be applied.

## G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 1. Gender assessment

A gender assessment for REPP2 was undertaken to explore the relationships between men and women, their access to resources and the constraints they face relative to one another in REPP2 Host Countries where there is a high risk of climate change and energy access.

The REPP2 investment mandate targets some of the most economically and socially vulnerable countries in Africa, with the majority (>50%) of the population living below the International Poverty Line in more than 13 countries. In many cases, there is some variation in how REPP2 Host Countries rank in terms of different gender indices, as outlined below:

- **Gender Development Index (GDI)**: which focuses on health, education and economic resources; Togo, Benin, Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, Liberia, Mali, Niger, Nigeria, Rwanda, Sierra Leone, Madagascar and Malawi all score higher on GDI value, approaching or exceeding the world average.
- **Gender Inequality Index (GII)**: which measures gender inequalities in reproductive health, employment, political empowerment and education. The GII is significantly lower for REPP2 Host Countries than the world average; and DRC, Niger, Nigeria and Sierra Leone are all below the sub-Saharan African average.
- **Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI)**: which measures gender parity across economic participation and opportunity, educational attainment, health and survival, and political empowerment. 11 World Economic Forum (WEF), 2022 Global Gender Gap Report 2022.


---
**Page 99**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 95

Compared with the sub-Saharan Africa as a whole (which ranks in the middle ahead of Middle East and North Africa, and South Asia), Cameroon, Lesotho and Madagascar rank above average, while most Host Countries remain on the lower spectrum on gender equity values.

According to a 2022 World Bank et al report on progress towards SDG 7, sub-Saharan Africa ranks the least in terms of access to electricity. This is because most countries lack sufficient data on electricity access in the region is limited, thereby making it difficult to assess how this lack of electricity access relates to gender; research by IRENA has shown that women-owned households and enterprises find it harder to access energy services.

While regional and country-level gender-disaggregated data for the energy sector remains scarce, research at the global level shows that the share of women employed in the renewable energy sector (32%) is higher than their representation in other sectors. In Africa, women’s participation in the renewable energy sector is substantially higher in non-technical jobs (30%-35%) than in STEM jobs (13%). While women's participation in management and leadership positions remains low, IRENA research shows that 42% of the boards of directors are women on the boards of renewable energy companies in Africa (27%) than on boards in general (Africa’s average is 18%).

At a global level, female renewable energy entrepreneurs are struggling to access the financing and business support they need to grow their companies. Although Africa has a higher share of female-owned businesses than anywhere else – which demonstrates the active and important role women play in the continent's economy – female entrepreneurs face significant challenges when it comes to accessing finance. According to IRENA, only 10% of African women entrepreneurs access business value chains.

### 2. Gender action plan (GAP)

Access to energy can help individuals, households and businesses to complete certain time-consuming tasks in their daily lives. Access to electricity may free up time from fuel collection and household tasks to enable longer periods of study time or other productive use of time, and thereby decrease gender gaps in key educational and economic areas. Additionally, access to energy for, example access to lighting, improves the quality of life by reducing the risk of injury and improving safety at home. Access to electricity can also improve women’s health through better sanitation and hygiene practices, and increase their opportunities for personal wellbeing.16 Further, in REPP 2 we believe that the funding of women-led and focused businesses will be a key driver of the energy transition and will help to accelerate the energy transformation change for generations to come. This transformative change, driven by gender focused financing, is crucial to overcoming the gender equity gap.

The following action plan outlines the steps that REPP 2 intends to take towards bridging the gender equity gap:

**Action 1: Integrate a gender equity perspective into the design, implementation, monitoring and assessment of all renewable energy activities financed by REPP 2. This means requesting all investors to integrate a gender lens in their project designs and business plans with support to promote gender equality within actionable targets to improve gender equality within their operations and project implementation will positively impact the industry and its beneficiaries. This involves:**

1. Analyzing gender equity in the investee company, country of operation, and targeted sector:


---
**Page 100**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 96

## Establishing an investee-specific GAP by identifying gender performance indicators and gender-disaggregated targets against an established baseline; and
## Integrating the outcomes of (1) and (2) into a monitoring plan.

### REPP 2 will provide investees with companies supporting in assessing gender baseline and establishing action plan. Such includes:
- Gender diversity training provided by renewable energy companies – example, provided via the REPP 2 gender toolkit.
- Gender mainstreaming training provided in form of webinars and REPP Phase I Academy (as described above). REPP 2 will provide REPP investees only as an example can be provided previous webinars and REPP Phase I Academy training (see annexes 2); and
- Individual support to investee from the REPP 2 Manager’s impact team in form of review of gender baseline and action plan.

### Action 2: Dedicate a target of 30% of all investments towards companies that meet 2X GLI criteria. Those are companies that:
- Are founded by a woman or women, or are majority-owned by women; and/or
- Have at least 30% women in senior management; and/or
- Provide products or services designed to disproportionately benefit women; and/or
- Have a workforce that is at least 30% women, together with a progressive human resource policy.

The funding can be:
- In form of early-stage growth funding for off-grid companies (Activity 1), development-stage funding for grid-connected projects (activity 2); finance for expansion of SHS businesses (Activity 3);
- In form of equity investments in renewable energy companies that meet the GLI criteria and are engaged in activities to promote gender equality (Activity 4) and can be in form of hybrid equity instruments or as senior or subordinated loans, subject to the company’s business plan.
- The activities are expected to lead to the following impacts:
    - Total female beneficiaries reached: 290,875
    - Total female beneficiaries adopting improved livelihoods and/or new climate resilient livelihood options: 134,600
    - Promotion of gender equity within renewable energy development companies by working towards investing US$70 million in companies meeting the 2X GLI criteria.
- Achievements: 15,890 jobs generated, improved access to information for 547,687 women and girls; and 26 developers with improved ESG practices (including GAPS).

The detailed gender assessment and action plan are presented in Annex 8.

## Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

REPP 2 will adopt and build upon the policy framework, governance and system architecture that REPP Phase I established and refined since its inception in 2015. Where necessary, this will be expanded and further refined to align with the GCF’s governance standards and will be overseen by Camco in its role as Accredited Entity.

REPP 2 will produce an annual business plan for investment and funding targets for the year, as well as an annual workplan providing on an activity basis. This will inform the budgeting process used to determine the activities required to execute the strategy for the coming year, leaving REPP 2 to effectively


---
**Page 101**
---

undertake activities and deliver value within the budgetary constraints set. The business plan will also summarise the previous year’s performance and any actions resulting from analysis of the results.

REPP will prepare monthly portfolio reports that include:

- Progress towards meeting programme targets for the platform
- Deals in documentation and updates on transactions as they move closer to being contracted and ready to disburse
- Position statements and disbursements in disbursements, accrued income, revaluations, impairments and disposals
- Metrics showing portfolio weightings and performance against targets and risk thresholds, as required (e.g., technology, country, off-taker etc.) and so on.

The portfolio reports will allow effective monitoring of the platform’s performance by the governing committees and stakeholders, ensuring corrective action is taken and assisting in developing strategy which strengthens the platform’s position within the market.

REPP will also prepare quarterly management accounts, comprising a profit and loss account, balance sheet and cash flow statement. These will be prepared in accordance with the relevant Generally Accepted Accounting Principles (GAAP) or other accounting standards under which the audited annual report and financial statements will be prepared.

Accompanying the Cash Flow Statement will be a Cash Forecast, prepared quarterly and providing a detailed view of expected cash inflows and outflows over the next 12-month period. It will also include a forecast of cash returns from the portfolio over the life of the platform.

REPP will engage a firm of accountants to undertake an external audit of the platform’s annual financial statements, in accordance with relevant GAAP or other accounting standards under which the audited annual report and financial statements will be prepared. The audit will provide a detailed view and give transparency to governing committees and stakeholders, highlighting areas of importance and anything which may require attention.

REPP will maintain separate bank accounts into which it will receive funds and make disbursements. The bank accounts will be reconciled in the REPP accounting system daily or on an ad-hoc basis. Disbursements will be controlled through a process that ensures all appropriate milestones have been completed for each transaction.

- Commissions prior to disbursement in applicable financial agreements have been met
- Payments for suppliers or service providers, service level agreements or valid contracts exist between the platform and beneficiary
- Platform policies on preventing fraud and mitigating sanctions are compliant with any platform policies and training requirements
- Beneficiaries have undergone appropriate KYC / AML and sanctions checks

Cash controls will be in place within REPP which include dual authority for setting up and approving payments, and which have payment limits for approvals or disbursements of different sizes. Material transactions will require sign-off from the Depository Agent.

REPP will adopt and apply the Accredited Entity’s approved procurement policy, which is already in use at REPP. The same policy will be applied throughout the Programme’s activities. Please refer to Annex 10: Procurement Policy & Plan for further details.


G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

- No confidential information. The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.

- **Redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.**

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.
