# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP222

## Proposal name: Renewable Energy Performance Platform (REPP 2)

Accredited entity: Camco-Management Limited (CAMCO)

Country/[ies]: Cameroon, Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Lesotho, Madagascar, Malawi, Niger, Nigeria, Sierra Leone and Zambia

Project/programme size: Medium

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

Note: This assessment report is based on a review of the updated and revised proposal received from the accredited entity (AE) for funding under the Renewable Energy Performance Platform (REPP 2). It follows the assessment report based on an earlier version of the funding proposal submitted by the AE for the third-party testing of the REPP, during which it has been reviewed and assessed by the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP). This current assessment report reflects the updated version of the AE's implementation in response to the earlier independent TAP assessment report for B36. It also offers a follow-up on the two video calls held between the parties on 9 August 2021 and 9 September 2023.

### 1.1 Overview

In its assessment, the independent TAP is guided by decision B.35/12 of the GCF Board, which outlines principles for demonstrating the impact potential of GCF-supported activities as well as other appraisal and assessment guidance in considering these investment criteria and as endorsed by the Board.

The Renewable Energy Performance Platform 2 (REPP 2; the fund) is a new USD 250 million Mened Fund financed facility established as an esedric on commitment specialise (SCS) or special limited partnership (SLP). The fund is managed by Camco-Management Limited (CAMCO).

The main purpose of the fund is to invest in the early-stage development and implementation of renewable energy projects, with a focus on small-scale renewable energy projects in sub-Saharan Africa, with a focus on least developed countries. The current list of target markets for REPP 2 includes Cameroon, the Democratic Republic of Congo, Lesotho, Madagascar, Malawi, Niger, Nigeria, Sierra Leone and Zambia.

The fund is a successor to a predecessor fund, the Renewable Energy Performance Platform 1 (REPP 1), which CAMCO managed from 2015 until 2023. The total commitment of USD 88 million sterling (GBP) in grant funding from the Foreign Commonwealth and Development Office (FCDO) was provided by FCDO to REPP 1, with a further USD 47 million in co-financing provided by FCDO having been drawn by REPP 1 and the fund is still active but no longer makes new investments.

The purpose of the current pre-funding proposal is to request GCF for funding in the form of joint equity participation in REPP 2 in the amount of USD 50 million, which CAMCO, as the AE, would invest alongside other investors. The proposed co-investors are the Global Environment Facility (GEF) and the World Bank Group (WBG). CAMCO serves as both AE and executing entity (EE) for this funding proposal.

5. The junior equity funded by GEF is expected to allow REPP 2 to: (i) leverage private, commercial and international financial institutions (IFI) funding tranches into its capital stock (both senior and junior); (ii) absorb the junior equity tranche into its capital stock; (iii) its portfolio would first be absorbed by the junior equity (and not part pence across all equity investors); (iv) allow for a greater degree of flexibility in the structure of the fund, such that if the remuneration of the junior equity tranche does not reflect risk-adjusted pricing.

6. The main purpose of REPP 2 is to drive a paradigm shift in the development of small-scale renewable energy projects in developing countries. This will be achieved through the following project types:

(a) On-grid scale renewable energy generation projects (independent power producers) with an installed capacity of up to 25 MW for small hydropower, and solar power projects and SMMW for wind (categories B and C risks only) and a target allocation of 10 per cent for SHS companies in the REPP 2 portfolio.

(b) Off-grid energy services based on renewable energy, including mini-grid operators and stand-alone systems. The target allocation is 50 per cent for mini-grids and 10 per cent for SHS companies in the REPP 2 portfolio.

7. REPP 2 will provide a range of financial instruments to invest in these projects, focusing on direct equity investment, hybrid equity, debt financing, and other similar instruments:

(a) Early-stage funding growth for off-grid companies. This would include hybrid equity (subordinated debt, convertible notes and other similar instruments) to support mini-grid operators and stand-alone systems as they scale up their operations, thus addressing the shortage of early-stage high-risk equity in the market.

(b) Development-stage funding for grid-connected projects. Similar to the above, this strategy would provide hybrid equity as well as subordinated or senior loans to enable them to move new renewable energy projects along the development cycle, with an option to convert into equity at a later stage if they are able to demonstrate that they have overcome the hurdles preventing them from moving forward.

(c) Expansion of SHS businesses. This would include equity as well as debt finance for SHS companies that want to grow their businesses and/or expand into new markets and those that need to access capital in order to scale up their operations, thus reducing their risk profile.

(d) Construction or mini-grid portfolio. This would include hybrid equity as well as subordinated or senior loans to mini-grid operators for finance construction of new facilities to grow their footprint and client base. This funding would also be for mini-grid operators that are looking to diversify into other markets, such as developing businesses models and underdeveloped/unregulated energy targets in emerging market countries.

(e) Construction finance. Through this strategy, REPP 2 would provide hybrid equity, subordinated or senior loans to finance construction of small-scale on-grid renewable energy projects where it finds it challenging to mobilize the required financing from the commercial market.

8. Apart from providing financial instruments to investors, REPP 2 will further support investors by offering a range of risk mitigation tools: (i) hybrid equity with embedded credit risk insurance and off-taker credit enhancement/risk insurance available in the market; (ii) development of strategies to mitigate hard currency risks for investors; (iii) development of

skills and capacities of local entrepreneurs and project developers; (iv) support to environment and social risk management by investors (e.g., providing assistance in the development of environmental and social management systems); and (v) promotion of gender equity (e.g., through the inclusion of gender equality and women-specific components, which will be mandatory for all projects).

* REPP 2 also features a technical assistance facility (TAF) that will: (i) support investors and entrepreneurs in preparing project development grants to enhance their project development capabilities; (ii) work with governments/registrars in target markets to develop a conducive enabling environment for the REPP 2 portfolio; (iii) provide capacity building services; and (iv) provide assistance to female entrepreneurs to help close the gender gap in the renewable energy market. The TAF will be funded by the Green Climate Fund (GCF), with an initial contribution in the amount of USD 5 million for the TAF is pending with GCF. The independent TAP comments the AIE for raising funds for the TAF from other sources.

* The REPP 2 project period is expected to last five years, and during which period it would invest in approximately 37 transactions (of which 26 are expected to be innovative precedents). In addition to the USD 10 million that has been committed by GCF and REPP 1 (co-funding as IFC leverage), the fund is expected to mobilize USD 150 million as co-financing from private sector investors, as well as development partners (e.g., multilateral agencies) and IFIs. The independent TAP notes that of the individual assets that the fund will invest in, REPP 2 expects to mobilize a total of USD 786 million in co-financing from private sector investors, with USD 150 million expected to come from private sector and commercial sources (i.e., IFIs' investing on commercial terms), translating into an overall leverage ratio for the fund of roughly 4:1. The independent TAP notes that the remaining 90 per cent of funds mobilized at asset level will be provided by development partners and IFIs, though the funding proposal does not provide details on the source of these funds.)

In terms of impact, the AE effects REPP 2 to achieve the following targets:

(a) 310 MW of new renewable energy capacity installed (translating into 135,580 MWh of electricity generation);

(b) 12.7 megatonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (Mt CO₂e) is avoided or reduced greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions (full REPP investment portfolio over technology lifetime);

(c) New or improved access to clean energy for more than 7.7 million people;

(d) Improved climate resilience for nearly one million beneficiaries; and

(e) 76,081 jobs (of which 23,940 jobs will be for females).

### Impact potential

Scale: Medium to high

The funding proposal credibly demonstrates that, if successful, REPP 2 has the potential to engender considerable mitigation, adaptation and sustainable development benefits in target countries. Overall, the theory of change, results framework and calculations/attributions

### Methodology for Assessing Overall Mitigation, Adaptation and Sustainable Development Impacts

The proposed methodology for assessing the overall mitigation, adaptation, and sustainable development impacts is transparent and based on best practice/market standards (see annex 2 B of the funding proposal). The independent TAP was able to reconstruct the high-level input data used in the baseline scenario and the reference case scenario as well as the data that were provided by the AE. The funding proposal also contains reasonably detailed assessments of climate change impacts, adaptation measures, and sustainable development co-benefits (see annex 2 of the funding proposal) that support the overall climate rationale for this funding proposal.

Regarding the expected mitigation impact, the overall GHG emission reduction/avoidance target over the lifetime of the programme is expected to be 17.2,290 t CO₂ eq. The calculation of this target is based on a combination of measures (e.g., energy efficiency, REPP across a mix of technologies/installations), the expected generation energy (126,721 MWh per year for the entire portfolio) and the relevant grid connection tariffs (per kWh). The overall mitigation impact will be achieved through a range of technologies including AMIS-IA electricity generation by the user (version 19); AMIS-ID grid-connected renewable electricity generation by the user (version 4); and REPP 2 large-scale hydropower generation (see energy version in section 4). In practice, the actual projects funded by REPP 2 will be likely be substantially different from those described above. The overall mitigation impact is expected to exceed the overall targets presented appear reasonable and achievable. All mitigation targets will be aligned with the Paris Agreement and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) of the target countries, underlining the relevance of investment in renewable energy capacity as a key mechanism for reducing GHG or avoid fossil fuel-based energy generation alternatives.

More specifically, REPP 2 is expected to contribute to climate change adaptation via three channels:

1. First, by investing in small-scale renewable energy generation (including off-grid businesses), REPP 2 is expected to create more resilient power systems by way of diversification and redundancy.
2. Diversification away from large-scale hydropower generation that is increasingly subject to heightened variability in water availability due to climate change impacts; and
3. Increased resilience through the development of microgrids, which are less reliant on the reliance of energy users on grid powers that are becoming increasingly unreliable due to extreme weather events and other disruptions.

Secondly, through investments in off-grid solutions (e.g., mini-grids, SHS companies), the fund expected to increase the climate resilience of people and communities (including micro, small, medium-sized enterprises) by providing them with access to renewable energy as an alternative to unsustainable fossil fuel/hydropower solutions. The total number of beneficiaries REPP 2 is expected to reach in excess to 370,224, based on its current pipeline.

While the fund is expected to contribute significantly towards achieving these positive climate outcomes under a best-case scenario, there are also various challenges that fund will face in meeting this target. These include:

18 One of the key factors driving the Fund's potential to have impact at scale is the ability of the AE/TC to mobilize significant co-investments from other funding providers beyond the requested amount of USD 20 million in public equity and USD 50 million in private equity/investors. In addition to raising USD 100 million in junior equity from public/captive investors (including the proposed REPP 2 investment), the Fund is also expected to raise an additional USD 100 million in senior equity and USD 50 million in notes (either from private sector, commercial and IPI funding sources, with a resulting leverage ratio of 1:1.5). The independent TAP notes various challenges that may arise when mobilizing co-investments beyond the requested equity and notes for REPP 2, specifically:

### (b) Time frame manager and risk profile: While the AE has experience with renewable energy investment in sub-Saharan Africa, the only reference is to the predecessor fund to REFP 2 (REFP 1), which was funded entirely by grants. (The independent TAP was informed that the AE had no experience of investing on a commercial basis or in industrial fund focusing on promoting solar investments; that fund is in the process of closing and has already committed to an average annual return of 8 per cent, with returns (5.1 per cent on portfolio, which is to some extent a consequence of the strong development of the market in South Africa). The AE also has experience as a financial counterparty (FCOJ). Consequently, private sector investors as well as DFIs that invest on a commercial basis will consider the EE as a de facto first-time manager. In this context, the size of the fund and its risk profile are important factors for private sector investors with a low risk profile (small-scale investment in countries with ratings of "B" or below) in an overall complex environment where there is a lack of liquidity and where the market is deteriorating (mainly fundamentals in FCF 2 target markets).

### (b) Returns for investors and liquidity options: The return expectations REFP 2+1 differs significantly from those of REFP 1, which was funded entirely by the fielded interested parties. In its dialogue with the independent TAP, the AE noted that a higher hurdle rate for the fund would be required to attract private sector investors (particularly in the private sector) but highlighted that this could also be achieved through a refinancing of the loan book at year 12. The AE also indicated that it was possible to close the loan book at year 12 which would provide investors (who typically would not want to lock in a funding commitment for more than five years) with an exit option. In addition, the independent TAP also considers crucial for the AE to attract especially private sector investors to REFP 2. The AE highlighted that there were expressions of interest from some potential investors but that these investors had not yet committed and would need to negotiate a guarantee (potentially through Swedish International Development Cooperation Agency or another party) in order to commit to an investment. A refinancing of the loan book will help. The independent TAP strongly encourages the AE to prioritize private sector investors, as this is crucial for REFP 2+1. While the independent TAP believes that, without certainty on early liquidity, the fund will have difficulty attracting private sector investors (particularly in the private sector), possibly enabled by a refinancing guarantee from the Swedish International Development Cooperation Agency or another party), REFP 2 would also make a significant contribution to the secondary market development, something that is very lacking in much of the African continent.

### (c) Fund life: The independent TAP notes that the fund life has been extended to 26 years a fund life up to 25 years, which the independent TAP deemed unlikely with view to raising capital. While the independent TAP recognizes that an extended fund life is required in order to meet the long-term investment horizon of the investors, it also believes that this would have further increased the risk profile of REFP for private sector as well as commercial (DFI) financiers. The independent TAP notes that the fund life could potentially become very large, representing a significant portion of capital commitments. (For clarity, the independent TAP notes that the fund life is not necessarily linked to the fund life up to 25 years, which addresses the challenge to some extent. The independent TAP also notes that the GCF benefits from a Most Favored Nation clause in the term sheet with respect to the fund life and that this could be used as an exit vehicle (e.g., because of negotiations with national public and private funder) to GCF.

### (d) Overall, the independent TAP believes that REFP 2+1 is likely to attract investments of this nature, leaving a narrow funding market. In the current macro environment, even DFI investing on a commercial basis may find it difficult to commit sizable tickets to REFP

2. The AE has provided a fundraising update to the Secretariat and the independent TAP that suggests, despite these overall challenges, there is some decent investor interest in the fund, as well as a reasonable response from DFSs and IFIs who are currently evaluating investment opportunities. As of 15 May, the AE had received commitments for investments totaling USD 60 million. While the independent TAP commends the AE for the significant efforts it is putting into fundraising, it notes that the current level of interest in the fund remains low. It also points out that there remains a risk that the AE will not be able to raise the entire USD 250 million target volume for the fund, which would have a negative impact on its ability to achieve its climate change mitigation and adaptation targets.
3. Translating additional generation/investment in off-grid components into actual use of clean energy: The main investment strategy of the fund, as noted above, is to invest in on-grid projects that will provide access to clean energy for households and businesses. However, the positive mitigation impact appears relatively straightforward: solar power systems in the target countries are currently being installed by small-scale renewable independent power producers, the additional renewable generation (and in some cases, storage) capacity created through this investment will be absorbed/used, thus avoiding use of fossil fuels alternatives. However, for investments in off-grid projects, the mitigation impact is more complex and less certain. This is because the fund implies in the funding proposal that investments in mini-grid operations and business wills by definition provide access to clean energy for households and businesses. The data from the baseline survey on the ability and willingness to pay in the target markets of REPP 2 show that many households and businesses are already paying for electricity, and that the fund’s investment offers due to the high initial connection charges (in particular for mini-grids, which is 40 per cent of the target charge) will not be attractive. As a result, it may be difficult for the fund to meet the credit profile expectations of most SHS businesses (10 per cent of the target allocation of REPP 2). It that indeed materializes, and investments don’t translate into new connections and use of clean energy, then this would have a negative impact on its ability to achieve climate change mitigation and adaptation targets.
4. The independent TAP is not specific to REPP 2, and addressing it in some extent outside its immediate sphere of influence, independent TAP notes with appreciation that the AE, in the revised funding proposal, makes a dedicated commitment (under activity 6) to work with its clients to ensure that investments are made in projects that will provide access to clean energy for all’s Universal Energy Facility), with particular focus on vulnerable groups and micro businesses. The independent TAP commends this approach as it is consistent with the AE’s active engagement in the design of various minimum revenue guarantee schemes relevant for mini-grids, and other off-grid investments. It also notes that the fund should consider providing connections that can then be leveraged to its clients. The independent TAP recognizes that REPP 2, which has a relatively low target volume, may not provide connection subsidies or minimum revenue guarantees from its own balance sheet.
5. Overall, the independent TAP regards the fund’s impact potential as medium to high.


1.3 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium

The main mission of REPP 2 is to deploy capital into a series of small-scale renewable energy generation projects (up to 73 transactions). The success (or impact) of the fund should thus lie in its ability to provide access to clean energy for households and businesses on-grid, considering its mitigation, adaptation and sustainable development outcomes as well as its commercial performance.

However, the funding proposal suggests that REPP 2 will also offer transformative potential that goes beyond the immediate impact of its investor portfolio, principally through three channels:

# First, the funding proposal highlights that RIPP2 will build local developer and business capacity, resulting in a larger and higher quality deal flow that will ultimately precipitate the growth of the local renewable energy industry beyond the fund and its portfolio.

## Second, the funding proposal notes that the increase in renewable energy capacity will result in enhanced energy security in the context of increased climate hazards, thus contributing to the achievement of the Paris Agreement goals.

### Third, according to the funding proposal, the use of clean energy produced by RIPP2 investors will trigger more income-generating activities, help improve social service delivery, and contribute to poverty alleviation.

#### The independent TAP recognizes the importance of these "transformation channels" as part of the broader impact of RIPP2. These channels are expected to have a significant impact (and thus paradigm shift) of RIPP 2 in the small-scale renewable energy market of its target countries will depend to a large extent on three interrelated factors:

##### The ability of RIPP2 to create and scale up viable business models that work for small-scale investors and the challenging market contexts that the fund will operate within. This is a model that could provide a basis for replicable scaling-up.

###### The ability of RIPP 2 to attract and leverage capital (as outlined in footnote 1 above) at the level of the fund on the back of a clear product offering and reasonable risk-return profile, which would be attractive to institutional investors. This is dependent on the extent to which the market understands the RIPP 2 product, and a crucial basis for any scaled-follow-on investments from the private sector; and

####### The ability of RIPP2 to attract and leverage capital from local capital providers (local commercial banks, pension fund managers, etc.) into projects that RIPP 2 will finance in the near term. This is dependent on the extent to which these investors believe what they are going to scale and become "self-perpetuating" (i.e., not reliant on international funding with concessional terms), the local funding market will ultimately have to come to the table.

##### On the first factor, in the funding proposal the AE emphasizes the importance of deploying a transaction advisory service that is tailored specifically for the projects in the target region (and has significant experience with this from RIPP 1). In the revised proposal, the AE provides some tangible examples of its approach to "project finance light," emphasizing the philosophy behind it and how it can be applied to the specific context of the proposed project. However, there is a tension between being flexible in tailoring bespoke solutions and, at the same time, having a clear product offering that can be scaled up for replication. This is what they could then adopt and scaled up by others. For fundraising with private sector investors, the focus should be on creating a "product" that is attractive to them (i.e., a product as they typically look for a clear and efficient "product suite") to be executed. Finally, the independent TAP notes that there are several ways of structuring deals in terms of projects to mature at later stage (as discussed above) if the transaction structures vary widely.

###### The AE has noted that the fund (based also on its experience with RIPP 1) will establish specific transaction advisory services that can be scaled up and replicated, which could lead to a proliferation of transaction models. The independent TAP welcomes this and encourages the AE, at point of scale-up, to share lessons learned from the pilot phase, including best practices of what types of transaction models and standardized documentation work and have potential for replication and scale. This could be of particular relevance in the words of the TAP, as it would allow RIPP2 to contribute more strongly to scaling up and strengthening technology deployment, innovation and market transformation.

28. On second factor, for reasons already highlighted above, the independent TAP believes that few private sector investors will consider investing in REP2. As a result, this will leave DFTs and IFIs (International Finance Corporation, African Development Bank etc.), as the main sources of funding for GCF investments in REP2. The reason is that the submarket hurdle rate (because of the submarket hurdle rate for junior equity investors) to these DFTs/IFIs rather than to private sector investors is lower than the expected return on investment for the proposed project and notes that, due to the difficulties of raising funding (especially from the private sector), it will be difficult to meet the GCF’s target of 20% of total capital commitments at the level of the fund. Also, the GCF investment criteria do not prescribe any co-financing requirements for that matter, private sector leverage targets for GCF investments. However, the independent TAP believes that the GCF should consider including a co-financing requirement as a precondition for it to effect paradigm shift. A scaling up of the scope and impact of the programme is possible if this is done. The independent TAP’s approach demonstrates its ability to leverage private sector investors into the capital structure of the fund.

29. In this context, the independent TAP welcomes the introduction of a tiered pricing structure for different investor categories in the revised and updated terms sheet (annex 14 of the funding proposal). This will allow the GCF to better match its investment strategy with available funds making available. If implemented, this would reflect the reality that private sector investors are unlikely to commit to long-term funding commitments to REP2 without attractive returns. Even considering the risk mitigation measures and other incentives provided by the GCF, such a return on investment could be considered excessive. The independent TAP recommends including a clear definition of private sector investors (following the definition in footnote 1 of this assessment report) to ensure that the terms sheet is consistent with the GCF’s investment criteria for private sector investments.

30. In order to ensure that REP2 can unfurl its full paradigm shift potential, the independent TAP recommends that the GCF should consider including a co-financing requirement as a precondition of this funding proposal. “Other than in relation to the first disbursement of the GCF Proceeds, delivery by the Accredited Entity to the Fund, in form and substance satisfactory to the Fund, of an amount not less than 20% (i.e., more than US$15 million) of the total capital commitments to CanRoP REP2 SCSP (including any amounts committed to CanRoP REPs), and delivery by the Accredited Entity to the Fund of an amount not less than one per cent (1%) of the total capital commitments to CanRoP REPS (including any amounts committed to CanRoP REPs), at the same time as the GCF Proceeds from the requested disbursement are delivered to the Fund. The co-financing requirement should be met by entities (i) where the majority of shares or ownership in that entity (i.e., more than 50%) is held by private sector investors, or (ii) that benefit from an explicit state guarantee or other government support agreement.

31. More broadly speaking, the mobilization of private sector funding for climate action is identified as a key challenge to achieving the GCF’s investment strategy. In this context, the independent TAP tries to focus on the specific private sector leverage targets (e.g., at portfolio level), or financial catalysts that can be used in conjunction with the funding proposal. However, setting a mobilization target into funding proposals is not the only way of mobilizing funds in the real world, and in the markets as we find it. We would suggest that the Secretariat explore ways to better understand how private sector investors are likely to respond to the GCF’s investment strategy. The independent TAP review. We would also suggest exploring incentive mechanisms for ATIs to ensure they can play a more active role in mobilizing private sector funding, including by leveraging GCF funding. GCF is not the only funding institution grappling with this issue; many public climate funds are facing similar challenges and are looking at various ways of mobilizing money by leveraging private sector capital. The independent TAP believes that, in order for GCF to ensure that it can meet its investment strategy, it should consider including a co-financing requirement in a manner fully aligned with a country-driven approach to meeting developing countries’ needs and the objectives of the USP (updated Strategic Plan for GCF), specifying leverage targets and

### Introduction of Incentive Mechanisms

The funding proposal suggests that at the level of individual assets, it expects to raise roughly USD $93 million (or 50 per cent of the total funds expected to be mobilized from private sector sources). The independent TAP is unclear about where the balance in mobilized funding would come from. The proposal also notes that the fund will support a range of transactions, including public-private partnerships, project development funding, concessional funding from development banks, and government contributions. Considering the complexity of the proposed transactions, it may be difficult to raise the required level of funding from private sector and commercial sources; however, the estimate is based on the assumption that roughly 50 per cent of the funds will come from private sector financing.

The independent TAP notes that strong leveraging at the asset level could even further increase the share of co-financing from financial intermediaries. The independent TAP also acknowledges that local private sector financing is harder to obtain and more expensive (than driving, for example, the development of a large-scale infrastructure project or a portfolio of small infrastructure projects), but the independent TAP believes it is essential to ensuring that the local funding community can participate in REPP 2. The independent TAP also notes that the inclusion of lending after 10-12 years via a commercial bank is an additional way to achieve the objective of involving private sector financing.

The independent TAP appreciates the differentiated and detailed treatment of the issues surrounding local currency financing by REPP 1 in the funding proposal. The independent TAP agrees that the fund will be able to provide local currency solutions, but it notes that this will depend on the level of individual assets. The independent TAP also recognizes that the fund needs to balance the need for a large part of its portfolio to be denominated in foreign currencies (thus generally favouring local currency financing) and, on the other hand, ensuring that the risk profile of REPP 2 remains manageable. This will partly depend on the sector involved: sectors mean carrying an intense currency mismatch at the balance sheet level. In actual practice, it would appear that for a large part of the targeted portfolio, the AE/UE will transfer the currency risk to the fund and thus the fund will be able to provide local currency solutions (the fund may not be able to carry the associated risk). The independent TAP encourages the AE also consider other ways to mitigate this risk. For example, it could use a swap or forward.

There may be alternative ways for the fund to provide local currency solutions in practice. In addition, the AE can consider using other financial instruments (such as guarantees) and/or working with the European Commission, Nordic Development Facility, and KfW Development Bank) to raise additional funds. The independent TAP notes that these are not new approaches but have been implemented very effectively in other blended finance funds such as the SANAD Fund, the European Fund for Southeast Europe, and the Green for Growth Fund. A sustainable approach to local currency financing is also being considered by the World Bank.

The AE proposes to engage with governments and regulators in key target markets with the aim of removing regulatory barriers or providing regulatory certainty (e.g., through consents/changes). The AE also intends to provide support to governments/regulators in target markets via the TAP (which is outside the scope of the proposal) to address enabling environment issues. In light of this, the independent TAP acknowledges that the fund will be able to engage with public sector counterparts and regulators in target markets. However, engaging in transactions in the target markets will be challenging and will require consistent engagement with public sector counterparts and regulators. The independent TAP also notes that some of the transactions that the fund will support will create a model for follow-on transactions to occur. For example, the fund may support a transaction under REPP 1 that is similar to the type of transactions in the REPP 1 portfolio and how such model transactions with REPP 1 involvement had been instrumental in triggering additional investment activity in the respective markets

1.4 Sustainable development potential

The funding proposal presents a credible pathway for REPP to 2 generate significant sustainable development benefits.

(a) Economic co-benefits: The funding proposal notes that the programme is expected to create approximately 300 jobs. The logic model provided by the AE (annex 22 of the funding proposal) as well as the explanations provided in the Q+4 section point to the reality that REPP 1 has already created a number of jobs, particularly through new generation capacity supported by REPP 2 investments. The funding proposal notes that "The total number of jobs created is estimated at approximately one quarter of jobs created by MW [megawatt] build, multiplied by MWs financed". These numbers are technology specific and have been driven from IRENA: Renewable Energy and Jobs. Annual Report 2018.

(b) Social co-benefits: The funding proposal highlights multiple social co-benefits resulting from programme investments, mostly due to improved access to lighting, the electrification of households, and the creation of new jobs. The funding proposal notes that those benefits (such like the economic co-benefits) only accrue if the investment in generation capacity is accompanied by a deliberate focus on gender equality and the use of clean energy by beneficiaries; and

(c) Gender-sensitive development co-benefits: Finally, the funding proposal argues that because women are disproportionately involved in small-scale renewable energy generation will disproporionately benefit women and girls as at present they tend to be responsible for managing household energy consumption. The funding proposal notes that access to electricity will enable them to use their time more productively (e.g. schooling). REPP 2 will also aim to ensure that the gender balance in the project pipeline is consistent with the 2:gender lens investing criteria. Because of the data diligence on the fund pipeline, REPP 1 has already demonstrated that it can meet this criterion and currently performs against the 2:gender lens investing criteria. However, in the funding proposal, the status quo at the end of 2012 phase 1 of REPP had committed $21m, which is 30% of the fund pipeline. The funding proposal notes that the gender balance in REPP will similarly be able to source 2:gender aligned deals; and

(d) Impact assessment: As part of the logical framework of the GCF term sheet, all impacts delivered through REPP 2 are captured in the logical framework, which forms the basis of all monitoring and reporting under the programme.

Overall, the independent TAP rates the sustainable development impact potential of REPP 2 as high.


1.5 Needs of the recipient

The funding proposal notes that the programme is expected to create approximately 300 jobs. The logic model provided by the AE (annex 22 of the funding proposal) as well as the explanations provided in the Q+4 section point to the reality that REPP 1 has already created a number of jobs, particularly through new generation capacity supported by REPP 2 investments.

## 1.6 Country ownership

### Coherence with existing policies: The funding proposal (annex 2) provides comprehensive evidence that REPP 2 is broadly aligned with the priorities of host governments in target countries.

- Nationally determined contributions and associated implementation targets;
- National climate policies;
- National energy policies and legislative/regulatory environment for private investment in renewables;
- General national development policies; and
- National gender policies.

### Capacity of the AE: Since 2015, the AE has been implementing REPP 1, a fund similar in objectives (investment in small-scale renewable energy generation) but with some notable differences. While REPP 1 is primarily focused on equity investments in the capital structure of the fund), REPP 2 invests by committing debt or equity products (but it cannot be limited to only commercial bank lending). REPP 2 also features an extended selection of target countries.


---
**Page 130**
---

## The independent TAP recognizes that the AE will be able to leverage many learnings from REPP 1 to REPP 2 but also views the significant changes in the capital structure (especially with expected participation of private sector and commercial investors) and the extended geographical coverage as a major challenge.

### Efficiency and effectiveness: Scale: Medium to high

As also noted in the funding proposal, the estimated net per Gt CO2 eq mitigated/avoided is relatively low compared to other REPPs (0.5–1.3 tCO2eq). This is not surprising considering that the investment focus is on small-scale renewable energy projects that typically feature higher fixed investment costs and lower operational efficiency leading to a higher level of cost and thus lower efficiencies with regard to emissions avoided/reduced.

The long fund life (20 years) and the likely implications of this for fundraising potential with private sector and commercial investors are also discussed in detail in the funding proposal. As a potential junior equity investor, GEFO would be at the first in last out of the fund, while private sector and commercial equity providers may find liquidity inferior (if a viable path is developed to exit the fund). The TAP notes that this could lead to an overvaluation of the counterfactual (what GEFO could do with the funds if the fund had been shortened).

The independent TAP assesses the efficiency and effectiveness of the funding proposal as medium.

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The independent TAP endorses this funding proposal.


---
**Page 131**
---

14. Overall, the funding proposal is ambitious and has potential to generate significant mitigation adaptation as well as broader sustainable development benefits for the target countries through projects that will receive investments from the fund. In focusing on the small scale renewable energy sector, the TAP also plays a key role in addressing the documented gap, especially in access to high-risk equity capital that is required by many of these projects. The TAP also has an important complementary role within the GCF as it focuses on least developed countries and thus on markets that have mostly seen little or no private investment in small scale renewable energy projects, is particularly welcome.

15. The independent TAP also wishes to highlight the importance of using a blend of capital stack (as was the case in REPP 1) into a blended finance structure that opens the potential for co-financing from private sources. In this regard, the TAP has engaged with CAMCO to explore the possibility of providing grant funding along private sector investors in particular markets and with investing in this new asset class where governments are unfamiliar to them. CAMCO has considerable experience in the target region and thus appears well positioned to provide such co-financing.

16. In order to ensure that REPP 2 can unfold its full paradigm shift potential, the independent TAP recommends to the Board to include the following condition to its potential approval of the funding proposal:

*Other than in relation to the first disbursement of the GCF Proceeds, delivery by the Accredited Entity of a Co-financing Agreement with private sector investors must be accompanied by written evidence confirming that Co-financing from private sources of an amount not less than ten per cent (10%) of the total capital commitments to Canco REPP SICp (including the requested disbursement) has been committed and/or is in place. Such co-financing should be provided by private sector investors who either: (i) have the majority of shares or ownership in that entity (i.e., more than 50%) are held by public sector entities; or (ii) that benefit from an explicit state guarantee or other government support agreement.*

# Executive summary

This gender assessment explores the relationships between men and women, their access to resources and the constraints they face relative to one another in REPP 2 target countries within the context of climate change and energy poverty.

REPP's target countries have made progress in integrating the gender dimension at the political, strategic and legal levels and in some indicators REPP 2 target countries rank better than the world average. However, there are still significant gaps to be addressed as women continue to disproportionately benefit from renewable energy infrastructure and services, as well as unequal chances to engage with economic opportunities presented by renewable energy access. The following key barriers have been identified:

- Women face hurdles accessing modern energy
- Women's employment opportunities in renewable energy are limited
- Women's access to funding at the individual and company level is limited

The Gender Action Plan (GAP) will be implemented through two main actions: 1) dedicate 30% of REPP 2 funding towards companies that meet the Zix Challenging Gender Lens Investing (Zx GLI) criteria, and 2) integrate a gender equality perspective into the design, implementation and monitoring of all renewable energy projects. The focus will be on addressing the needs of women and girls as well as other vulnerable groups of the population, as well as overcoming the existing inequalities that exist in the renewable energy sector, which are presented in more detail in Section 4.

## Overview of the target countries

REPP's investment mandate will extend towards some of the most economically and socially vulnerable countries in Africa, most of which fall into the low human development category under the 2021 Human Development Index (HDI); specifically, Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Madagascar, Malawi, Niger, Nigeria, Sierra Leone and Lesotho. Despite Cameroon making it into the medium human development cohort, it is still ranked out of the top quintiles and frequently falls below group averages on key development indicators as presented below in Table 1.

**Page 138**

Indices presented in Tables 1 and 2 place RPP 2 target countries among the poorest in the world, with women being disproportionately disadvantaged in terms of the opportunities and benefits that the countries and their economies have to offer, which results in lower values in general development indicators for women compared to men. This highlights the importance of gender-disaggregated data for the assessment of women's empowerment and poverty reduction.

Lack of gender-disaggregated data is especially disturbing in generalised indicators such as the World Bank’s International Poverty Line. Recent studies show that the way the indicator is calculated fails to consider differences between households, which are often based on different levels of consumption and income variables: consumption and income. The values for these variables are obtained through household surveys, which provide information on consumption and income at the household level, whereas statistics on the poverty line are usually calculated by dividing national poverty lines by the number of household members; individual indicators are calculated by dividing household-level data by the number of household members.

The World Bank recognises that this approach involves several important assumptions, such as that household resources are shared equally across family members; that family members have identical needs; that two individuals in a household consume and spend twice as much as one person living alone; or that there are no differential costs of reaching a given welfare level per person for households of different sizes. These assumptions do not correspond to realities, as gender inequalities often make women and girls more vulnerable than men to poverty. They also fail to take into account the unequal distribution across household division of assets, work and responsibilities, and power relations that drive these disparities.

With this in mind, RPP 2’s gender assessment and respective GAP have been drawn up based on available gender-disaggregated data, which has allowed us to place women and men in the context of their respective countries and communities and to assess gender inequalities pertaining to the energy sector. The data sources

# Gender gap

Gender equality is measured by a number of international indexes, as presented below. Despite relative variability, African countries generally seem to have the lowest scores not only in terms of human development, but also gender equality and other related indicators.

This assessment considers the most recent indicators from UNDP Human Development Reports, World Economic Forum (WEF) Global Gender Gap Report 2020, and the latest edition of the Gender Inequality Index. These sources provide a comprehensive overview on gender issues of the region. The WEF report provides an overall score for each country in terms of gender equality, while the UNDP Human Development Reports address the role of women in the energy sector (as service providers, employees, and end users), since they lack sufficient country-level statistical data. Where such data is not available, the assessment herein has been made on generalised data for the region and other sectors (such as industry as opposed to energy) as well as latest available country-level data obtained from other verified sources.

## 3.1 Indicators and indexes

### Gender Inequality Index (GII)

The GII measures gender inequalities in three basic dimensions of human development: health (based on life expectancy at birth), education (expected years of schooling and mean years of schooling) and command over economic resources (GNI per capita).

### Gender Inequality Index (GII)

The GII measures gender inequalities in the three basic dimensions: reproductive health, empowerment and the labour market. The two main gender inequality indexes calculated by UNDP in its human development report are:

- **Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI)** benchmarks the current state and evolution of gender parity across four key dimensions, namely economic participation and opportunity, educational attainment, health and survival, and political empowerment.

### 3.2 Health and wellbeing

Despite the progress that has been made in recent years (see Table 5), HNP target countries still experience high levels of maternal mortality, with a rate as low as their respective life expectancy at birth compared to world averages.

Health practices such as gender-based violence and child marriages are also persistent across the countries, which lack sufficient legal basis for the protection of women. With the exception of DR Congo and Malawi, all of the target countries have legislative exemptions regarding the minimum legal age of marriage, since the consent of the parents is required to marry. In some cases, this is only applicable when marriage is governed by the customary law as well as the statutory law; the Registration of Customary Marriages and Divorce Act 2007 allows for underage customary marriages if they happen with parents' consent<sup>**c**</sup>.

# 3.3 Education

According to the available data, relative progress has been made in REPP 2 target countries and the rest of Sub-Saharan Africa with regards to overall access to, and the attainment of, education by both men and women. At the same time, this progress has not been significant enough for the indicators to catch up with world averages and other regions.

Fewer than half of REPP 2 target countries (Cameroon, Lesotho, and Madagascar) have female literacy rates comparable to those of the male population while also closely approaching or exceeding world averages. In other countries, women are at least 20% less likely to be literate, with only 27% of Niger's adult females being able to read and write with understanding a short simple statement about their everyday life, as presented in Table 7 below.

Low literacy rates have a direct correlation with educational attainment by boys and girls. Across REPP 2 target countries, 40% of fewer adult females aged 25 and older have reached (but not necessarily completed) a secondary level of education, as presented in Table 8. The actual number of adults that have ultimately completed secondary education is even lower: only 13.7% for Sub-Saharan Africa in 2012, with a persistent gap between males (30.2%) and females (27.1%).

The Global Education Monitoring Report 2022 includes survey results of secondary school-age adolescents or their parents in Malawi, Nigeria and Sierra Leone showing their reasons for not being in school.<sup>18</sup> Among those

When it comes to tertiary education, Sub-Saharan Africa is performing poorly with regard to gender parity, with women still remaining at a disadvantage, as compared to the rest of the world where gender disparity in tertiary education exists due to the expense of men. The ratio of women to men that enrolled at tertiary level education for the region remained between 0.6 and 0.8 between 2000 and 2020, while the world average value increased from around 1.7 to 3.5 during this period.

Despite regional averages, some REPP 2 target countries have shown significant increases in the female/male tertiary education enrollment ratio, however. According to the available data, the adjusted gender parity index value has improved significantly between 2000 and 0.9 in 2020 in Benin, almost reached 1.0 in Madagascar and remained around approximately 1.4 in Ethiopia.

Nevertheless, for other REPP 2 target countries, such as Niger, Malawi and DRC, female enrollment in tertiary education remains almost two times lower than male enrollment, as evidenced by data presented in Table 9.

Out of the few women who do enroll in tertiary education in Sub-Saharan Africa, most opt for traditionally more "female" fields, such as arts and humanities or business, administration and law. According to the data collected in the Tertiary Education Survey 2012 and presented in Tables 9 and 30, STEM specialties, as well as engineering, manufacturing, construction and natural sciences are significantly underrepresented by women compared to a significantly lower female enrollment rate compared to the male one.

Gender stereotypes and socio views on traditional gender roles in Sub-Saharan Africa have been shown to play an important role in determining both men's and women's choices. A 2019 IFC report, for example, found women who enrolled in technical or engineering programmes were viewed unfavorably.[**38**]

All of the above contribute to the disadvantaged position of women when considering employment in the energy sector. Women’s literacy rates and general lack of education plus the low STEM enrolment rates of women are among the factors that hinder women from entering this field.

3.4 Economic opportunities

### 3.4.1 Employment

In most REEP 2 target countries, working-aged women are less likely than men to engage in the labour market, either by working or actively looking for work, as presented in Table 11. Female unemployment is also generally higher than male unemployment and has increased compared to 2014 indicators, likely as one of the negative consequences of the COVID-19 pandemic.

At the same time, the female labour force participation rate in REEP 2 target countries remains significantly higher than the world average of 46.2%, with the gap sometimes exceeding 30 points, such as in Madagascar where the rate is 81.5%. This corresponds to the overall trend for Sub-Saharan Africa and highlights the importance of addressing women's employment challenges in these countries.

Women tend to be engaged as contributing family members or through informal arrangements. The share of informal employment among women in REEP 2 target countries is high and for women it is recorded to be higher than for men, with 89.7% of female workers employed through informal arrangements as compared to


---
**Page 147**
---

82.7% of male workers.37 At the same time, available country-level data presented in Table 12 indicates that in Malawi and Sierra Leone a slightly bigger share of men are informally employed compared to women. Nevertheless, the International Labour Organisation (ILO) argues that women in the informal economy are still more likely than their male counterparts to be working in precarious conditions, such as domestic work, for instance as domestic workers, home-based workers or contributing family workers, than their male counterparts.38 For instance, in some REPV 2 target countries the gender gap in being engaged as a family worker is particularly large. In Malawi and Sierra Leone, for example, women are more likely to be family workers than men (54% vs. 17%).39 In DRC, 26.2% of female workers contributed to the household income as family workers in 2019 compared to 5.3% of male engaged in the same type of employment. It is remarkable that in Malawi and Sierra Leone a particular strong gender gap exists between women and men in contributing family worker’s plot and contribution to the production of both cash crops and subsistence farming.40 The burden of household responsibilities that fall overwhelmingly on women makes them more likely to jeopardize their jobs and careers.41

### 3.4.2 Access to land

Women's ability to participate in a labour market, especially as entrepreneurs or own-account workers, as well as their ability to access financial services largely depends on ownership of assets, and real estate in particular.

Asset ownership has a multidimensional impact on the life and economic empowerment of women. The ability to administer assets and use them for productive gain purposes, the ability to use the assets as collateral for credit or as a resource base for starting a business by enhancing liquidity through rental or sale, independence


---
**Page 149**
---

from male family members and having confidence in the ability to make a living are important prerequisites for women’s economic empowerment.

Assets ownership in Africa and involves the issue of women's legal ability to own real estate. As described in Section 3.6 below, three REEP countries (Cameroun, DRC and Niger) significantly limits women's rights to land ownership, inheritance and grants a husband all administrative rights over assets owned by married women.

In practice, available statistical data, presented in Tables 14 and 15, shows that there is a significant disproportion in the amount of land owned and/or effectively managed by men and women in REEP target countries. This is particularly true for agricultural land where women are often excluded from decisions making major decisions regarding resource use and exercises management control over the agricultural holdings, not necessarily due to ownership rights. In addition, even though distribution of agricultural landownership by gender varies across countries, it is clear that in most cases, men own more land than women (even when recorded to be women), the area of land actually owned solely by women and the value of the land is statistically smaller than the area and value of land owned solely by male landowners, as presented in Table 15.

In addition, even within the countries where women are legally granted equal rights to own and administer the property, they often face practical obstacles that establish a de facto discrimination against women governed by customary law, which operates in parallel to statutory law and often disadvantages women.

### 3.6 Legal status of women and gender stereotypes

All REEEP target countries have made significant improvements in promoting gender equality in law. Based on the World Bank Group study, DRC is the world's top 10 most improved economies since 1970 in relation to gender equality in law. Furthermore, the statutory law does not differentiate between genders in granting individual rights to a person in all of REEEP’s target countries.

Nevertheless, women are disadvantaged by a vast number of areas due to their persistent de-valuation by local customs and gender stereotypes. The effects of customary law, and even more so its statutory law, Common restrictions that prevent economic development and empowerment of women in REEEP's 12 target countries are further described in Table 18 below.

# Restriction

## Description

Gender-based discrimination with regards to access to credit is not prohibited in Cameroon, Madagascar, Malawi, Niger, Nigeria, and Lesotho.

### Assets

A number of restrictions on property ownership by women in is place in RPP2 countries. In many cases, women are excluded from the right to inherit or dispose of land which formed their wealth or own/inherit property, especially land. Also, in some countries, women do not have equal rights with men and law recognizes a husband's ultimate decision-making power with regard to administering assets.

Examples of the type of restrictions women face include:

- In Cameroon, both civil and customary legal regimes do not allow married women to inherit or dispose of property. Instead, all real estate is considered private property is under the authority of the husband who may manage and make decisions about it. The law also recognizes a husband's ultimate decision-making power with regard to land; women are often regarded as the property of the husband.
- In DRC, no distinction is made in the provision of land rights between women and men under statutory law. Yet under customary law, traditional chiefs, who have the authority to allocate land, give preference to male heirs when allocating land. By these principles, inherited land is almost always given to sons rather than daughters or other female relatives.
- In Malawi, women are not allowed to inherit property from their parents and also, given deeply embedded stereotypes surrounding the roles of women and men in society, it is more likely for a woman to be allocated land by her husband. It is more likely to see joint land titling among unrelated men than husband and wife.
- In Zambia, where customary land accounts for the majority of land (96%) and governed by traditional authorities, only men have the right to own and inherit customary land; while wives or children may only have access to it.

According to the 2019 Social Inclusion and Gender Index's country profiles, RPP2 target countries for which data is available have medium (DRC, Lesotho) to very high gender-based discrimination in social institutions.


---
**Page 154**
---

which beyond the structural and legal barriers also include norms, attitudes, stereotypes and fundamental customs and traditions restricting women’s independence and economic empowerment.76 SIGI 2021 Regional Report for Africa (77) summarizes the role played by these discriminatory social institutions in women's access to employment and other economic opportunities as follows:

* Discriminatory social norms that restrict women’s education also limit their women’s access to the labour market. In addition to an increased burden on women in the form of unpaid domestic and care work, the proportion of people declaring that it is not acceptable for a woman in her family to do any paid work outside the home has risen from 2015 to 2019. The perception that earning money and providing for the family is considered an integral part of masculine identity, and not seen as favourable in women.

* Perception of women’s abilities and discriminatory educational norms tend to confine women to certain occupations or jobs in specific sectors of the economy. In Africa, men tend to be overrepresented in “manual” sectors such as construction, mining and quarrying or transport, storage and communication. Women are predominant in sectors such as accommodation, wholesale and retail trade or household services. This is not a coincidence but rather a reflection of social norms and practices that takes its origins from the choice of educational path and vocational training. The implications for economic empowerment are far-reaching, as women in Africa seem to be largely drawn to sectors that are not conducive to their economic independence.

* Men’s traditional status and roles, household decision-making practices and discriminatory inheritance practices limit women’s ownership of agricultural land and constrain their economic independence. Customs and traditions related to land ownership are deeply rooted in African societies and often in practice supersede formal civil or common law as REF 2 target countries.

* Various discriminatory social norms and biases related to access to markets, finance, training and technology also contribute to the gender gap in energy access. These discriminatory bases result in limited attempts by women to even enter the credit market due to self-perceived lack of creditworthiness, as well as in actual constraints over access to credit due to lack of sufficient economic resources, lower income and high informality rates in employment.

### Gender inclusivity in the energy sector

Energy, especially electricity, is an invaluable basic commodity that the quality of people’s livelihoods is indispensably dependent on. From the gender perspective, the energy sector can be viewed as an important provider of the resource base necessary for the everyday life and economic activity of either the household or individual.

# 4.1 Women as employees

The energy sector is dominated by men. Women are underrepresented in the sector. It is also a relatively small sector in Sub-Saharan Africa and therefore not a big employer on the country- and region-wide scale compared with other sectors such as agriculture, industry, or services.

African Development Bank has estimated that in 2019:

* **Electricity:** 1.8% of GDP
* **Gas and water activities:** 1.3% of GDP

Total employment in the sector constitutes a small percentage of total employment in the target countries, ranging from less than 5% in Malawi to more than 40% in Sierra Leone.

The concentration of the already limited number of jobs on offer in the sector by gender shows significant disparities, with female employment generally not exceeding one third of total sector employment and dropping to one tenth of the sector workforce.

Despite the currently insignificant value added by the energy sector to the GDP of African countries, the renewable energy projects are more likely to attract women professionals than traditional energy projects.

The positive effects of such investment included not only an increase in electrification levels for rural and remote communities but also enhanced opportunities for the economic empowerment of women.

# 4.1.1 Female employment profile in renewable energy companies

The share of female employees across Sub-Saharan Africa is higher in non-technical departments, such as administration and finance. In contrast, women make up only a small proportion (5%) of technical STEM and non-STEM departments; they have only a 13% and 16% share of female employees respectively, according to IFC research.[7]

Women are more likely to be employed in renewable energy companies that provide solutions in small-scale, decentralised energy systems (37% of full-time employees) compared to large-scale, on-grid, centralised energy systems (22% of full-time employees).[8]

When looking at executive positions, women comprise only 20% of chief executive officers (CEOs), 16% of board members and 9% of senior managers in renewable energy companies across Sub-Saharan Africa. Comparing CEOs across sectors, McKinsey found that 6% of African CEOs are women,[9] while a UN study found that 3.5% of women from the leading publicly traded companies in G-20 countries hold CEO positions.[10]

Women make up only 8% of executive-level employees and 27% of board members in renewable energy companies in Africa (27%) than the G-20 country average (20%) and the African average (18%). By comparison, women comprised only 6% of the executive-level employees and 21% of board members in energy utilities in Sub-Saharan Africa in 2019.[11]

# 4.1.2 Employment barriers for women in renewable energy industry

Barriers to women's participation in the renewable energy sector exist both at the recruitment stage and at career progression. At the same time, there is a notable difference in the perception of these barriers by men and women. According to IFC research, only 30% of women working in renewable energy companies in Sub-Saharan Africa, only 28% of men - compared to 40% of women - believed that women faced more barriers than men when seeking to enter the sector. The gap increased further to 60% when considering the perceived barriers advancing women's careers in the sector, with 45% of interviewed women and only

# Workplace policies and practices, including:

- The absence of effective recruitment and promotion policies with specific gender diversity targets, which would exclude gender biases;
- The lack of access to field work, which impedes women's career advancement by limiting their opportunities to increase their understanding of renewable energy value chain and technical matters;
  - Gender pay gap;
  - Sexual harassment and violence in the workplace; and
  - The lack of access to training and learning opportunities.

## Social norms and gender stereotypes, which select girls and boys from an early age, often impede their potential for future employment in any sector, not just renewable energy. Such norms and stereotypes relate to traditional gender roles in society and at the household level and are linked to low educational attainment rates, high school dropout rates, and a lack of women's access to technical roles, the unfavourable nature of female employment, and the increased burden of unpaid household duties and responsibilities.

## Legal barriers, which relate to restrictions of women's involvement in the work force. Such restrictions exist at both national level as well as such as the right of a husband to make decisions on his wife’s employment, which is afforded to men in Cameroon, or indirectly through lack of prohibition of gender-based discrimination in employment, as is the case of Nigeria. Labour legislation in a selection of countries, including Kenya, Tanzania, Uganda, Rwanda, Burundi, Ethiopia, Malawi, Mozambique, Sierra Leone and Lesotho, also contains more specific restrictions on women's employment in dangerous and industrial jobs, which constitute core business functions in the renewable energy sector. It is argued that denial of access for women to opportunities in these jobs impedes their ability to reach leadership positions.

# 4.2 Women as energy users

Interconnections between the empowerment of women and the energy sector exist not only in employment opportunities afforded to women in the sector, but also in electricity access and its impact on the everyday life of women.

It is widely recognised that where physical energy access is translated into actual use of energy services, it can help reduce women's poverty by (i) time saving (by substituting for manual labour) and enhancing convenience and safety; (ii) reducing the use of polluting energy sources; (iii) improving health through better lighting opportunities for new income-generating options or for enhancing existing ones; (iv) building social capital in education and health, a precondition for women's empowerment; and (v) providing access to information and entertainment.


---
**Page 159**
---

However, in Africa access to electricity is limited due to the absence of sufficient infrastructure and energy, even where such infrastructure is physically available the stability of the grid is often vulnerable. Furthermore, marginalised groups, including women and poor people, are often hindered in their use of modern clean energy by lack of access.

According to a World Bank report, Sub-Saharan Africa remained the world’s least electrified region in 2019 and accounted for 77% of the global population lacking access to electricity. More than one third of REPP target countries are located in countries with the largest deficit in energy access: Nigeria (92 million people), DRC (72 million), Niger (26 million) and Madagascar (38 million), with Nigeria and DRC being world leaders in the total number of unserved people.

While availability of country-level gender-disaggregated data on electricity access in Sub-Saharan Africa is limited, a few studies have been conducted. For example, in Zambia, female-headed households were more likely to use off-grid solutions than male-headed households. Similarly, in Niger, female-headed households were less likely to have electricity access even through grid connection or off-grid solutions.

The primary reasons for the lack of electricity connectivity also differed between the countries. In Niger, for both female- and male-headed households, the main reason for not being connected to the grid was that the grid was too far away or not available (82.4% and 67.4%, respectively), as presented in Table 24. This is especially true in rural areas where, among female-headed households, 91.3% of them reported that male-headed households would be unable to pay for the connection cost compared with their male counterparts (8.9%) versus 6.2%). This is especially true in urban areas, where only 31.7% of female-headed households reported the connection cost as a barrier.

In Zambia, both male-headed and female-headed households identified “grid too far or unavailable” as the main reason they were not connected to the grid. At the same time, as presented in Table 26 below, female-headed households found the connection cost was a bigger barrier for female-headed households than for male-headed households.

Regarding cooking technologies, higher disparities were present in Niger, where female-headed households were slightly more likely to use three-stone stoves and less likely to use traditional stoves[^10^] compared to male-headed households, while there was no gender gap in the use of LPG stoves. In Zambia, male-headed households were significantly more likely than female-headed households to use LPG stoves.

At the same time, in both countries women aged 15 and older spent considerably longer cooking or in the cooking area (more than two hours per day) in Niger and on average 3.6 hours per week in Zambia) than men, girls, or boys. Women are thus more likely to be affected by indoor air pollution.

The above findings highlight the importance of global and regional efforts to address fuel wood access and the reliance on solid fuels for cooking and other household energy-demanding chores – as well as informal economic sector activities – has a higher toll on women, contributing to their “time poverty” and health issues.[^13^]

For classification of cookstoves in Niger, a traditional stove is defined as basic wood stoves, charcoal burning stoves, and three-stone stoves. LPG stoves are defined as those that use liquefied petroleum gas (LPG) for cooking combustion temperature due to poor insulation, and a lot of cold excess primary air because of too many openings.

### 4.3 Limited funding to women-owned/led companies

Africa has a higher share of female-owned businesses than other regions, yet women entrepreneurs still face multiple challenges accessing the finance and support they need to grow their businesses. There is an estimated USD $20 billion annual gap for African women entrepreneurs across business value chains.

In 2021, less than 1% of funding raised by startups in Africa was directed towards female-led enterprises (18%), with most venture capital funding were considered gender-diverse.

Based on Global Banking Alliance's review of various government and private sector lending models, there are only a few examples that have been specifically to increase access for women owned micro, small and medium enterprises. In 2017, only 16-20% of women in West, Central, East and Southern Africa had access to long-term financing from financial institutions.

This is further confirmed by residents' understanding of the Investment Manager, which suggests that currently there are no specific policies or programs directly targeting: (1) African women entrepreneurs in the clean energy space or 2) businesses that specifically address women's needs through their business model design.

# Gender Action Plan

REPP 2’s investment mandate extends toward some of the most economically and socially vulnerable countries in Africa, with the majority (85%) of the population living below the International Poverty Line in more than half of the target countries. The REPP 2 Gender Action Plan (GAP) summarizes the actions that need to be implemented at the facility level to ensure gender equality considerations are integrated at the investment level.

As discussed above, gender gaps exist across key aspects of society—from economics and politics to health, education, and energy access. Women in many countries face barriers to accessing education and affordable clean energy, yet they are often burdened with domestic chores that prevent them from pursuing education or income-generating activities. Access to energy can help individuals, households and business to complete certain time-consuming tasks more efficiently, freeing up time for other productive uses. In addition, access to electricity and household toilets for longer periods of study time or other productive use of time, decreasing gender gaps in key educational and economic areas. Also access to energy, through access to lights, information, mobile phones are also important.

Furthermore, there is limited funding available to female-owned or female-led businesses in the region’s renewable energy sector. REPP 2 is taking direct action to increase the number of renewable energy companies that are aligned with 2X Challenge's Gender Lens Investing (2X GL) criteria by allocating a portion (minim 30%) of its investments to investees that meet these criteria, and/or operate in sectors or provide products and services that better serve women’s needs. REPP 2 also works with its investees to integrate gender equality perspectives into the design, implementation, monitoring and assessment of all of their renewable energy initiatives.

We believe that funding women, empowers them to be invested and build companies, teams, products and markets they see transformative for the generations to come. This is a meaningful step to overcome the gender equality gap.


## 5.1 Actions

Two main actions have been identified to bridge the gender equality gap:

### Action 1: Integrate a gender equality perspective into the design, implementation, monitoring and assessment of all renewable energy activities financed by REPP 2.

This means requesting all investees to put in place gender equality GAPS (Activity is Non-financial support to promote gender equity) with actionable targets to improve gender equality throughout their operations and implementation will positively impact the industry and its beneficiaries. This involves:

- Analysing gender equality in the overall company operation of, targetted sector;
- Establishing a Gender Equality Action Plan (GEAP) by identifying gender performance indicators and gender-disaggregated targeted targets against an established baseline; and
- Integrating the outcomes of (1) and (2) into a monitoring plan.

REPP 2 will provide investees with supporting in assessing gender base line and establishing action plan. Such support includes:
