---
**Page 163**
---

Gender equality toolkit for renewable energy companies - as an example can be provided REPP’s current gender toolkit:

I. Gender mainstreaming training provided in form of webinars and REPP academy (i.e., closed training sessions for REPP member only – as an example can be provided previous webinars and REPP Academy training see appendix 2);
II. Individual support to investee from the Investment Manager’s impact team in form of review and guidance;
III. Funding: growth and development funding (activity 1 and 2) can be used to finance the establishment of sub-project GAP by investee, where relevant.

The REPP 2 investees will form gender action plans at two levels:

- Corporate in which the review and action plan focuses on leadership commitment, support for gender equality and women’s and vulnerable individuals’ empowerment and ensuring non-discrimination and equal opportunities. Guidance for establishment of the GAP at corporate level will be provided in a REPP 2 gender toolkit.
- Project in which the review will focus on gender-specific risks at the project level, ensuring stakeholder consultation and engagement with women’s groups, women’s civil society organisations (WCSOs) in relevant areas, gender advocates, and women’s civil society organisations (appendix 1 provide list of organisations to consider). The review should also identify any structural barriers that may prevent women from accessing resources or opportunities at the project level. Guidance for establishment of the GAP at corporate level will be provided in a REPP 2 gender toolkit. The chapter 10 of the annexes 6 ESM give guidance to include stakeholder consultation and engagement with women’s groups, women’s civil society organisations (WCSOs) in relevant areas, gender advocates, and women’s civil society organisations (appendix 1 provide list of organisations to consider). The review should also identify any structural barriers that may prevent women from accessing resources or opportunities at the project level. Guidance for establishment of the GAP at corporate level will be provided in a REPP 2 gender toolkit.

Growth and development funding (activities 1 and 2) do not require the development of a project level GAP for ESMs as this funding is only intended to help the project to prove its feasibility. However, growth and development funding (activity 1 and 2) can be used to finance the establishment of GAP (and/or ESMs). See below figure.

Figure 1. Gender sensitivity screening for growth and development funding

Gender Assessment

**Page 164**

The GAP submitted by investee companies together with the Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) prior investment reaching financial close. Therefore, both ESMS and GAP are conditions precedent prior funding for construction funding (activity 4 and 5) and expansion funding (activity 3).

Figure 2. Gender sensitivity screening for construction and expansion funding.

Action 2: Dedicate a target of 30% of all funding towards companies that meet ZX GLI criteria. These are companies that:

- Are founded by a woman or women or are majority-owned by women; and / or
- Have at least 30% women in senior management; and / or
- Provide products or services designed to disproportionately benefit women; and / or
- Have a progressive human resource policy, including gender equality policies with a progressive human resource policy.

The funding can be in: from of early-stage growth funding for off-grid companies (activity 1), development-stage funding for grid-connected projects (activity 2), finance for expansion of SMS businesses (activity 3), finance for construction and expansion funding (activities 4 and 5) or finance for the purchase of assets (activity 5) and can be in from of debt, equity or hybrid instruments, subject to the company's funding needs.

The above actions are further defined in Table 25 along with indicators that will be monitored and the expected targets. For ex-ante target setting the simple assumption has been made that the 30 % ZX GLI aligned funding will lead to a 10% increase in women at senior management level, a 10% increase in women on boards, a 25% increase beneficiaries at household and business level to be women in line with the ZX GLI Criteria. This is anticipated to be a conservative assumption as at household level female to male ratio is typically 50 - 50.

In line with REPPF monitoring plan, gender indicators, actions and results are monitored and reviewed and where relevant this action plan or its underlying actions and targets are updated accordingly.

# Women's Employment

Women have an under-recognized role as energy consumers, producers and as energy entrepreneurs. This provides a good foundation for their engagement in mini-grid projects.

The business case for women’s employment can be made both at the HQ and the community level:

- **Performance:** Firms with greater diversity and presence of women are likely to exhibit extended stronger performance and return on investments.
- **Innovation:** Companies with a larger footprint of female employees are likely to be more innovative (and bring those ideas to market). Women score well as motivators and liked communication (e.g. motivating others and inspiring their teams).
- **Improved Service Delivery:** Workforce diversity roots a company in its customer base and can improve service delivery.
- **Safer Operating Environment:** Diverse teams can enhance the safety culture and ensure protocols are followed.
- **Community buy-in:** Enhanced community engagement and employment can create greater social license to operate.

### Opportunities to enhance talent pool and achieve equality

|Mindsia’s community mini-grid deployment in India created approximately 986 jobs. These jobs include:|
|---|---|---|---|
|180 direct grid-on-grid jobs, 131 full-time equivalent jobs for maintenance work, and 675 productive-use jobs through additional entrepreneurial activities.|

# Women's Employment

## Education
- Increase girls and women’s exposure to the energy sector (e.g., on-site internships)
- Mentoring and connection to role-models
- Creating a study environment, and looking at bias in curricular and in teaching
- Working with parents

## School-to-work transition
- Designing vocational programs
- Engaging students early into school-to-work transition career choices and through exposure to the sector
- Job advertisements: looking at language and company image
- Assess bias in recruitment process and engaging men
- Setting quotas

## Retention
- Addressing biases in workplace and work culture (e.g., policies)
- Customizing care responsibilities (e.g., travel distances, working hours)
- Paid family leave
- Comprehensive workplace facilities
- Address gender wage gaps
- Sexual harassment

## Advancement
- Standardizing job performance reviews and skills assessments
- Offering effective career progression training
- Providing opportunities for advancement
- Addressing and discussing discrimination

# Understanding Customers and Productive Uses of Energy

Mini-grid companies have an inherent interest in local consumers and the utilisation of locally-generated power.

With access to energy services, local entrepreneurs, micro-enterprises, and farmers can generate local jobs and income, and enhance livelihoods. However, given inherent gender-gaps at the community level, specific attention needs to be paid to ensure that women’s voices are equally distributed.

Barriers to equitable distribution of benefits include:

- Women have inequitable access to resources, such as land, collateral, and finance, indicating a need for tailored approaches (e.g. mobile money, subsidies, ready boards).
- Women are involved in different enterprises and access to livelihoods given cultural expectations and norms.
- Cultural norms, limited participation in community and household decision-making.
- Women have fewer opportunities to attend trainings and have less access to information given different mobility patterns.
- Unequal labor burden in terms of household and care work (unpaid) and fuelwood collection which leads to time poverty.

A deeper understanding of the women’s segment can help a company increase its customer base and market size, enhance loyalty and manage risks (e.g. payment, community ownership).


---
**Page 178**

# Understanding Customers and Productive Uses of Energy

## Prepare
- Staffing and budgeting for understanding customers
- Who to engage (e.g., female farmers, business owners, male partners, community leaders)
- How to engage (e.g., FGDs, interviews, community mapping, and mini-surveys)
- What to engage on (e.g. energy needs and value chains, willingness to participate in research)
- When to engage (e.g. times, location, partner consent, season etc.)

## Engagement and data collection
- Finalize specific research approach, methods, and tools to undertake data collection
- Test and finalize data collection tools (questionnaires, surveys) to collect data
- Areas may consider collecting data on include:
  - Women's role in key sectors and value-chains (e.g. agriculture)
  - Women's ability to do current tasks and reduce labor/costs and meet energy use aspirations
  - Women's willingness to pay (e.g., seasonal income with crop earnings etc.)
  - Access to finance, credit, loans, and appliances adoption
  - Access to information and business development opportunities
  - Identify key stakeholders that women trust or that could be engaged to form partnerships

## Develop actions and monitor
- Identification of key gender gaps with regard to demand and supply
- Prioritize the most pressing needs and possible issues to address
- Verify findings at the company level through workshops/discussions
- Commit to company action, set targets, allocate budget, and monitor progress

# Risk Management: Gender-based Violence

Gender-based violence (GBV) is an umbrella term for any harmful act perpetrated against a person based on socially ascribed (i.e., gender) differences between males and females. It includes acts that cause physical, sexual, mental harm or suffering, threats of such acts, coercion, and other deprivations of liberty. These acts can occur in public or in private spaces. Common forms of GBV include sexual violence (rape, attempted rape, unwanted touching, sexual exploitation, and sexual harassment), domestic violence, forced marriage, female genital mutilation.

Violence against women is a manifestation of historically unequal power relations between men and women, which have led to domination over women by men, such as economic dependence on men and to the prevention of the equal advancement of women (UN Declaration on the Elimination of Violence Against Women 1993).

| Workplace Sexual Harassment | Sexual Exploitation and Abuse (SEA) |
| --- | --- |
| Unwanted sexual advances | Exploitation of a vulnerable position, differential treatment, or pursuit for sexual purposes. |
| Request for sexual favors | Actual or threatened sexual physical invasion |

Company and Project-Site Risk


---
**Page 182**
---

# Risk Management: Gender-based Violence

## Assess Project Design
- Social risk assessment of community-level risks (child marriage, gender norms, girls drop-out rate)
- Assess labor influx and potential impact on project timelines
- Assessment of services for survivors (e.g., clinics, NGO's national hotlines)
- Review existing capacity to respond to risks
- Use risk assessment tool and development safeguard instruments
- Reflect on and update risk assessments during project implementation

## Address Project Implementation
- Establish mitigation, reporting, and monitoring measures
- Based on risks identified, identify the corresponding mitigations and adopt GBV policies and Code of Conduct and ensure these (including community participation procedures, trainings, engagement, etc.) are raised awareness
- Monitor the effectiveness of mitigation strategies and adapt/make changes as appropriate

## Respond Project Implementation
- Provide essential services for survivors
- Case route through the survivor's continuum (survivor-centered)
- Cases brought to closure documented and closed (focus on intake process)
- Check in with community (hire an expert)

# Risk Management: Gender-Based Violence

- Can you share any reflections on the possible GBV risks involved in mini-grid construction and operation? Do you think the risks of GBV are increased?
- How long will construction take? What is the level of labor hired for this project? Are employees transient and working away from families?
- Have discussions taken place with community and local groups that focus on the safety of women, children, and vulnerable adults at the project site?
- Are community members likely to provide goods or services to workers and contractors (e.g., entertainment venues, food, materials)?
- Are there any schools near the project site? If yes. What age are students?
- Have you as a company put in place any GBV mitigation and response measures?
- What will be the supervision arrangement at site during construction? How hard is supervision (e.g., remote, in a FCV location etc.)?
- Is there anybody with a background in GBV that could assists with the GBV related work in your company?

### Additional questions:
- Have discussions taken place with community and local groups that focus on the safety of women, children, and vulnerable adults at the project site?
